# NEW: Admin — GET /admin/jobs — List All Jobs (status, upload size, device info)
# Product: MayasabhaXR — backend (Node + TypeScript)
# Scope: New Feature / Backend APIs — admin/ops listing
# Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline
# Track: Backend APIs
# Priority: Medium

---

## Task Description

Add `GET /admin/jobs`: an ADMIN endpoint listing jobs across ALL users/projects with operational fields (status, upload size, device info, owning user) for monitoring, support, and ops. Unlike the user-facing endpoints (which enforce ownership), this is admin-gated and deliberately UN-scoped by ownership — an authorized admin sees everything.

Defining differences from the user endpoints:
- **Admin authorization (not just auth):** requires an ADMIN role/permission. A logged-in NON-admin MUST be rejected. Enforce strictly.
- **Cross-user:** lists all jobs, no ownership scoping (admins see all users' jobs).
- **Operational fields:** status, upload size (bytes), device info (manufacturer/model/OS), owning userId, timestamps, attempts, failure info.
- **Filtering + sorting + pagination:** filter by status/user/date, sort, and paginate (cross-user is large).
- **Sensitive:** admin access to all users' device/upload data — audit-log access; gate strictly.

- [ ] `GET /admin/jobs` under an admin namespace; ADMIN authz (role/permission), not just authentication
- [ ] List all jobs cross-user (no ownership scoping); operational fields (status, upload size, device info, userId, …)
- [ ] Filtering (status, user, date range) + sorting + pagination
- [ ] Audit-log admin access; reject non-admins strictly
- [ ] Operational fields sourced from the ingested job data (upload size + device info from the manifest/ingestion)

## Files to Inspect First

> Adjust these paths to the real repo layout. They reflect the assumed conventions in the Assumptions section — confirm before the agent edits anything.

1. P1 auth — the token/claims; whether an admin role/claim exists (or how admin is determined: role claim, allowlist, separate admin auth). The admin authz mechanism to build on
2. The job model + ingestion task — where `uploadSize` + `device` info are stored on the job (populated at ingestion from the manifest); the fields to surface
3. The processing-pipeline task — the job `status`/stage, attempts, error for the DTO
4. The user job endpoints (retry/history) — the base job DTO to extend with admin/operational fields
5. Existing list/pagination conventions (P1) — the envelope + paging/sort/filter patterns
6. Any audit-logging utility — to log admin access

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Admin authz (strict)
- Gate `GET /admin/jobs` behind ADMIN authorization — a role/permission claim (or the app's admin mechanism), NOT mere authentication. A logged-in non-admin → rejected (403, or 404 to hide the admin surface — confirm the convention). Confirm how admin is determined (role claim / allowlist / separate admin system) and enforce it in middleware.

### Step 2: Cross-user query (no ownership scoping)
- Query ALL jobs (across all users/projects) — do NOT apply the user-ownership filter. This is the intentional inverse of the user endpoints. Include the owning userId/projectId in the result.

### Step 3: Operational fields
- Map each job to an admin/operational DTO: jobId, projectId, userId, status/stage, attempts, uploadSize (bytes), device (manufacturer/model/OS), startedAt/completedAt, error (if FAILED), outputRefs (if COMPLETED). Source `uploadSize` + `device` from the ingested job data (populated at ingestion from the manifest).

### Step 4: Filtering + sorting + pagination
- Support filters: by `status` (e.g. all FAILED/PROCESSING), by `userId`, by date range (created/completed). Support sorting (e.g. by date, size). Paginate (cursor/offset per P1). Validate filter/sort/paging params (Zod). Stable ordering across pages.

### Step 5: Audit + safety
- Audit-log admin access (who accessed, when, filters) — admin listing of all users' data is sensitive. Return only the operational DTO (no unnecessary PII beyond what ops needs). Ensure the endpoint is under `/admin` and not reachable via the user routes.

## API Contract (illustrative)

```
GET /admin/jobs?status=FAILED&userId=&from=&to=&sort=createdAt:desc&limit=&cursor=   (ADMIN required)
 200 -> { items: [ { jobId, projectId, userId, status, attempts, uploadSize, device: { manufacturer, model, osVersion }, startedAt, completedAt?, error?, outputRefs? } ], nextCursor? }
 403 -> not an admin (or 404 to hide)
 400 -> invalid filter/sort/paging params
```
Rules: admin authz (not just auth); cross-user (no ownership scoping); operational fields (status, uploadSize, device, userId); filter/sort/paginate; audit-logged; sourced from ingested job data.

## What NOT to Do

- Do NOT allow non-admins — strict admin authz (role/permission), not mere authentication.
- Do NOT apply user-ownership scoping — admins see ALL jobs cross-user.
- Do NOT expose this via the user-facing routes — keep it under `/admin`.
- Do NOT skip pagination — cross-user listing is large.
- Do NOT omit the operational fields (status, upload size, device info) — the endpoint's purpose.
- Do NOT skip audit logging — admin access to all users' data is sensitive.
- Do NOT surface PII beyond what ops needs — the operational DTO only.
- Do NOT compute upload size / device info ad hoc — source from the ingested job data (manifest).

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Non-admin (authenticated) → rejected (403/404 per convention); unauthenticated → 401.
- [ ] Filter by status (FAILED/PROCESSING/…) → only matching jobs.
- [ ] Filter by user / date range → correct subset.
- [ ] Large result set → paginated; stable ordering across pages.
- [ ] Jobs missing uploadSize/device (older/incomplete ingest) → field null/omitted, not a crash.
- [ ] Mixed statuses across users → each accurate in the DTO.
- [ ] Invalid filter/sort/paging params → 400.
- [ ] Admin access → audit-logged.
- [ ] Soft-deleted jobs/projects → included/excluded per admin policy (admins may need to see them — confirm).

## Constraints

- MUST require ADMIN authorization (role/permission), not mere authentication; non-admins rejected strictly.
- MUST list all jobs cross-user (no ownership scoping) with operational fields (status, uploadSize, device, userId, …).
- MUST support filtering (status/user/date), sorting, and pagination with validated params + stable ordering.
- MUST source uploadSize + device from the ingested job data; handle missing fields gracefully.
- MUST audit-log admin access and expose only the operational DTO (no unnecessary PII).
- MUST live under an `/admin` namespace, separate from user routes.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] `GET /admin/jobs` returns all jobs cross-user (admin-gated) with status, upload size, device info, userId, and other operational fields.

- [ ] The endpoint is under `/admin`, not reachable via user routes.

## Assumptions

- **Admin authz (KEY):** Assumes an admin role/permission mechanism (role claim in the token, allowlist, or separate admin auth). Confirm how admin is determined and the reject code (403 vs 404-to-hide).
- **Operational fields source:** Assumes `uploadSize` + `device` are stored on the job at ingestion (from the manifest). Blocked/partial if ingestion doesn't populate them; confirm.

- **Scope:** The admin job-listing endpoint (admin authz, cross-user, operational fields, filter/sort/paginate, audit). NOT the user endpoints (prior), NOT ingestion (adjacent; populates the fields), NOT admin actions on jobs (separate).
- **Stack:** Node + TypeScript; admin authz middleware; the job model + ingested fields. Adjust to the repo's structure.

Generate a coding prompt for this task: Admin: POST /admin/jobs/:jobId/rerun — trigger reprocessing — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Backend APIs

================================================================================================>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>



# NEW FEATURE: Admin — POST /admin/jobs/:jobId/rerun (Trigger Reprocessing)
# Product: maya-viewer (Backend Processing Pipeline)
# Scope: New Feature (Backend API — admin/operational)
# Priority: Medium

---

## Task Description

Implement `POST /admin/jobs/:jobId/rerun`, an admin-only endpoint that re-triggers processing for an
existing job — typically a FAILED job to retry after a fix, or a COMPLETED job to regenerate the model.
It resets the job into the processing flow and re-enqueues it onto the processing queue, reusing the job's
already-uploaded source images and manifest (no re-upload). It must respect the pipeline's state-machine
and single-writer invariants rather than poking state fields directly.

- [ ] Restrict to admin/authorized operators only (not regular job owners)
- [ ] Load the job; allow rerun from terminal states (FAILED, COMPLETED) per policy; guard non-terminal states
- [ ] Reset the job into the processing flow (state → QUEUED) and re-enqueue it to the processing queue
- [ ] Reuse existing source images + manifest in S3 (do NOT require or trigger re-upload)
- [ ] Handle prior artifacts/error on rerun per the agreed reprocessing policy (replace vs version — see Assumptions)
- [ ] Be idempotency-safe so a double-click doesn't create two concurrent reprocessing runs

## Files to Inspect First

1. The job model and status enum — confirm states (QUEUED/PROCESSING/COMPLETED/FAILED), the `artifacts` and
   `error` fields, and which transitions are legal. Rerun re-enters QUEUED; confirm the allowed source states.
2. The processing worker (pull/validate/start) — confirm the message shape it consumes and the QUEUED→PROCESSING
   guard, so rerun enqueues a message the worker will correctly pick up and the reset state passes its guard.
3. `finalize` and its enqueue logic — rerun re-enqueues the same job; reuse the SAME queue and message shape
   `finalize` used, so the worker treats a rerun identically to a first run.
4. The completion (`completeJob`) and failure (`failJob`) tasks — rerun must clear/handle the prior terminal
   result (artifacts on COMPLETED, error on FAILED) consistently with those single-writer operations, ideally
   via a shared state-transition helper rather than an inline field write (see Assumptions).
5. The admin auth mechanism — find the existing admin/role gate (distinct from regular project-access auth) and
   reuse it. If no admin authz exists, that's a prerequisite gap (see Assumptions).
6. The stale-PROCESSING reclaim policy (flagged in the worker task) — rerun of a job stuck in PROCESSING
   overlaps with reclaim; confirm interaction.

### Tech note (task-specific)
Validate `jobId` (and any body) with Zod. Reuse the existing job-status enum and the SAME queue + message shape
as `finalize`. Reset state via the pipeline's state-transition path (or a shared helper), not a raw field poke,
so single-writer invariants hold. Gate behind ADMIN authorization, not regular owner access.

## API / Data Contract

Endpoint: `POST /admin/jobs/:jobId/rerun`
Auth: ADMIN/operator role required.

Path param: `jobId` (string)
Request body (optional): `{ "reason"?: string, "force"?: boolean }`
  - `reason` — audit note for why the rerun was triggered (recommended for admin actions)
  - `force` — allow rerun from an otherwise-guarded state per policy (confirm if needed)

Success response (200):
{
  "jobId": string,
  "state": "QUEUED",
  "rerunOf": { "previousState": "FAILED" | "COMPLETED", "at": string },
  "enqueued": true
}

Error responses:
  400 — malformed jobId/body
  401/403 — not authenticated / not an admin
  404 — job not found
  409 — job not in a rerun-eligible state (e.g., already QUEUED/PROCESSING) — see Edge Cases
  500 — enqueue/persistence failure (job not left in an inconsistent reset-but-not-enqueued state)

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Admin authorization
Gate the endpoint behind the ADMIN/operator role via the existing admin authz mechanism — NOT the regular
project-access check. A normal job owner must not be able to call this. 403 for non-admins.

### Step 2: Validate and load
Validate `jobId`/body (Zod). Load the job; 404 if absent.

### Step 3: Guard rerun eligibility
Confirm the job is in a rerun-eligible state:
- FAILED → eligible (retry after fix).
- COMPLETED → eligible (regenerate) per policy.
- QUEUED / PROCESSING (in-flight) → NOT eligible by default (409) to avoid two concurrent runs; allow only with
  explicit `force` per policy, and even then coordinate with the in-flight run (confirm — see Assumptions).
Decide and implement the eligibility rule explicitly.

### Step 4: Reset terminal result per reprocessing policy
Before re-enqueueing, handle the prior terminal result consistently with the single-writer operations:
- FAILED → clear/reset the `error` as part of the transition (the rerun supersedes the failure).
- COMPLETED → handle prior artifacts per the agreed policy: REPLACE (rerun overwrites the same artifact key on
  completion — ties to the deterministic-key/overwrite decision) or VERSION (keep old artifacts, new run
  produces a new version). Do not leave stale artifacts pointing at a job that's being reprocessed in a way
  that misleads consumers mid-rerun (see Assumptions).
Perform the state reset via the pipeline's transition path/helper so invariants hold — not a raw field write.

### Step 5: Reset to QUEUED and enqueue atomically
Transition the job to QUEUED and enqueue a processing message (same queue + shape as `finalize`) as a
consistent unit — mirror the finalize discipline: do not end in QUEUED-but-not-enqueued or enqueued-but-not-reset.
Use a conditional transition so a concurrent/duplicate rerun doesn't double-enqueue.

### Step 6: Audit and respond
Record an audit entry (admin identity, `reason`, previous state, timestamp) per the project's audit convention.
Return 200 with the reset state and rerun context.

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT expose this to regular users — admin-only.
- Do NOT re-upload or require re-upload of source images; rerun reuses the existing S3 bundle + manifest.
- Do NOT poke `state`/`artifacts`/`error` fields directly — go through the pipeline's transition path so
  single-writer invariants and atomicity hold.
- Do NOT allow rerun of an in-flight (QUEUED/PROCESSING) job by default (avoid concurrent runs); gate behind
  explicit `force` + coordination if allowed at all.
- Do NOT introduce a new queue or message shape — reuse what `finalize`/the worker use.
- Do NOT leave the job reset-but-not-enqueued (or vice versa) on partial failure.
- Do NOT add a new job-status string.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Rerun a FAILED job → error cleared, state → QUEUED, enqueued once; worker reprocesses cleanly.
- [ ] Rerun a COMPLETED job → prior artifacts handled per policy (replace/version), state → QUEUED, enqueued.
- [ ] Rerun an in-flight (QUEUED/PROCESSING) job → 409 by default; with `force`, coordinate so two runs don't
      race (confirm whether force cancels the in-flight run first).
- [ ] Double-click / duplicate rerun requests → conditional transition ensures ONE reset+enqueue; the second
      is a no-op/409, not a second concurrent run.
- [ ] Source images/manifest no longer present in S3 (expired/cleaned up) → rerun should detect this and refuse
      (can't reprocess without the bundle) rather than enqueue a doomed job. Confirm whether to validate bundle
      presence at rerun time (see Assumptions).
- [ ] Job not found → 404; non-admin → 403; malformed jobId → 400.
- [ ] Enqueue fails after state reset → 500, and the job is not left QUEUED-without-enqueue (roll back or
      rely on idempotent retry to reconcile).
- [ ] Stale PROCESSING (crashed run) being reclaimed AND an admin rerun arriving → coordinate so they don't both
      re-enqueue; the conditional transition should make one win.

## Constraints

- Admin-only authorization, distinct from regular job-owner access.
- State reset uses the pipeline's transition path (single-writer invariants preserved); no raw field pokes.
- Reset-to-QUEUED and enqueue are a consistent unit (no divergence), with a conditional transition preventing
  double-enqueue under concurrent/duplicate calls.
- Rerun reuses the existing source bundle + manifest; no re-upload.
- Prior terminal result (artifacts/error) is handled per the explicit reprocessing policy (replace or version).
- Same queue + message shape as `finalize`; existing enum; configured clients; no new infra/status.
- Admin actions are audited (who, when, why, previous state). No secrets in logs.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] Endpoint is admin-only; non-admins get 403 and cannot trigger reruns
- [ ] Rerunning a FAILED job clears the error, sets state → QUEUED, and enqueues exactly one processing message
- [ ] Rerunning a COMPLETED job handles prior artifacts per the agreed policy and re-enqueues
- [ ] In-flight jobs are not rerun by default (409); `force` behavior (if implemented) coordinates against
      concurrent runs
- [ ] Duplicate/concurrent rerun requests result in exactly one reset+enqueue (no double reprocessing)
- [ ] Rerun reuses the existing S3 bundle + manifest; no re-upload is required or triggered
- [ ] State reset goes through the pipeline transition path; single-writer invariants and atomicity hold
- [ ] Enqueue failure after reset → 500 with no QUEUED-without-enqueue inconsistency
- [ ] Missing source bundle (if validated at rerun) → refused, not enqueued as a doomed job
- [ ] An audit record (admin, reason, previous state, timestamp) is written
- [ ] Job not found → 404; malformed jobId → 400
- [ ] No new queue/message shape or job-status string introduced
- [ ] No TypeScript errors (`tsc --noEmit` passes); lint clean
- [ ] Code reviewed against "What NOT to Change" — no raw field pokes, admin-gated, single enqueue path

## Testing Instructions

1. As an admin, rerun a FAILED job; assert 200, state → QUEUED, error cleared, exactly one queue message, and
   that the worker picks it up and reprocesses. Example:
   `curl -X POST $BASE/admin/jobs/$JOB_ID/rerun -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_TOKEN" -d '{"reason":"fixed pipeline bug"}'`
2. As an admin, rerun a COMPLETED job; assert artifacts are handled per policy (overwritten on completion, or a
   new version created) and the job re-enqueues.
3. As a NON-admin (regular owner) token, call rerun; assert 403 and no state change / no enqueue.
4. Attempt rerun on a QUEUED/PROCESSING job; assert 409 by default; if `force` is implemented, verify it
   coordinates against the in-flight run (no two concurrent runs).
5. Fire two concurrent rerun requests for the same job; assert exactly one reset+enqueue.
6. If bundle-presence is validated: delete the source objects and rerun; assert refusal (not a doomed enqueue).
7. Mock an enqueue failure after reset; assert 500 and no QUEUED-without-enqueue state.
8. Confirm an audit record is written with admin identity, reason, and previous state.
9. Rerun not-found and malformed jobIds → 404 and 400.
10. Run `tsc --noEmit` and lint; confirm clean.

## Assumptions

- Assumed the standard backend stack (Node/TypeScript, Zod), the same queue + message shape as `finalize`, and
  an existing ADMIN authorization mechanism distinct from regular access. If no admin authz exists, it is a
  prerequisite — flag rather than inventing an ad-hoc gate.
- Assumed rerun is eligible from terminal states (FAILED, COMPLETED). In-flight jobs (QUEUED/PROCESSING) are NOT
  rerun-eligible by default to prevent concurrent runs; confirm whether `force` should cancel the in-flight run
  first or be disallowed entirely.
- Assumed the reprocessing-artifacts policy needs an explicit decision (this is the "re-complete with different
  pointers" case the completion task flagged): REPLACE (deterministic key overwrite; completion supersedes prior
  artifacts) OR VERSION (retain prior artifacts, new run creates a new version, GET returns the latest). This
  must be reconciled with the GLB-upload key decision (overwrite vs versioned) and the completion task's
  "already-COMPLETED with different pointers" handling — they should all follow the same policy.
- Assumed source images + manifest persist in S3 and are reused; if a lifecycle policy may have expired/cleaned
  them, rerun should validate bundle presence and refuse if gone. Confirm whether to validate at rerun time.
- Assumed state reset should go through the same transition path/helper the pipeline uses (so `completeJob`/
  `failJob`-style invariants hold). If those are separate operations, a shared "resetToQueued" transition helper
  may be the cleanest addition — confirm.
- Assumed admin actions are audited per an existing convention; if none exists, a minimal audit record is in scope.
- Assumed rerun coordinates with the stale-PROCESSING reclaim mechanism so a reclaim and an admin rerun don't
  both enqueue; the conditional transition is the shared guard.

Generate a coding prompt for this task: Admin: GET /admin/jobs/:jobId/download — signed URL for raw photo bundle — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Backend APIs

===================================================================================================>>>>>>>>>>>>>



# FEATURE: Admin Endpoint — GET /admin/jobs/:jobId/download (Signed URL for Raw Photo Bundle)
# Product: Internal / Backend Processing Pipeline
# Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline
# Track: Backend APIs
# Scope: New Feature
# Priority: Medium

---

## Context

This is an internal admin-only endpoint. When a capture job's photos are
uploaded to S3 (under `projects/{projectId}/jobs/{jobId}/`), authorized
Anthropic/MayasabhaXR admin users need a way to download the raw photo bundle
for QA, debugging, photogrammetry re-runs, or manual inspection — without
needing direct S3 console access.

The endpoint returns a presigned S3 URL pointing to a ZIP archive of all raw
photo files for the job. The ZIP is generated on-demand, cached in S3 for a
short TTL, and the presigned URL expires after a configurable window.

This endpoint must never be accessible to regular authenticated users —
admin-only middleware is the primary access gate.

---

## Task Description

- [ ] Add `GET /admin/jobs/:jobId/download` route behind an admin-only auth
      middleware
- [ ] On request, check if a pre-built ZIP for this job already exists in S3
      at a known cache key; if yes, return a fresh presigned URL for it
- [ ] If no cached ZIP exists, list all photo-type files for the job from S3,
      stream them into a ZIP archive, upload the ZIP to S3, then return a
      presigned URL
- [ ] Add `generateJobPhotoBundle()` to `src/services/bundleService.ts` (new
      file) — owns the list → zip → upload flow
- [ ] Add `generateAdminPresignedDownloadUrl()` to `src/services/s3Service.ts`
      — generates a short-lived presigned GET URL for the ZIP object
- [ ] Write structured logs at each phase: cache hit, cache miss, zip start,
      zip complete, presign
- [ ] Emit an analytics/audit event: `admin_bundle_downloaded` with `adminId`,
      `jobId`, `projectId`, `fileCount`, `bundleSizeBytes`, `cacheHit`

---

   `src/routes/admin.ts` and register it in the main router under `/admin`

   middleware; if it does not exist, create it in this task (see Step 2);

3. `src/services/s3Service.ts` — existing S3 client, `buildS3Key`, and

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Define Bundle S3 Key Convention

The cached ZIP lives at a predictable, deterministic S3 key so cache-hit
detection is a single `HeadObject` call with no DB state needed:
projects/{projectId}/jobs/{jobId}/bundles/photos.zip

Add this key builder to `src/services/s3Service.ts`:

```typescript
/**
 * Canonical S3 key for a job's raw photo bundle (ZIP).
 * Deterministic — same input always produces same key.
 * Bundle is considered stale after BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS.
 */
export function buildBundleS3Key(projectId: string, jobId: string): string {
  return `projects/${projectId}/jobs/${jobId}/bundles/photos.zip`;
}
```

### Step 2: Create or Verify Admin Auth Middleware

If `src/middleware/adminAuth.ts` does not already exist, create it:

```typescript
import type { Request, Response, NextFunction } from "express";

/**
 * Admin-only middleware.
 * Validates the request carries a valid admin token.
 * Apply this to every route under /admin — never skip it.
 *
 * Strategy: checks for a static ADMIN_API_KEY in Authorization header.
 * Replace with role-based check if user roles are added to the User model.
 */
export function adminAuthMiddleware(req: Request, res: Response, next: NextFunction): void {
  const authHeader = req.headers["authorization"];
  const adminKey = process.env.ADMIN_API_KEY;

  if (!adminKey) {
    res.status(503).json({ error: "Admin access is not configured on this server" });
    return;
  }

  if (!authHeader || authHeader !== `Bearer ${adminKey}`) {
    res.status(403).json({ error: "Forbidden — admin access required" });
    return;
  }

  next();
}
```

If a role-based system already exists on the `User` model (`user.role ===
"admin"`), use that pattern instead — inspect `src/middleware/auth.ts` and
extend it with a role check rather than a static key. Document which approach
was used in a comment at the top of the file.

### Step 3: Add S3 Helpers to `s3Service.ts`

Add the following functions. Use the existing `s3Client` instance — do not
instantiate a new one.

```typescript
import {
  HeadObjectCommand,
  GetObjectCommand,
  ListObjectsV2Command,
  PutObjectCommand,
} from "@aws-sdk/client-s3";
import { getSignedUrl } from "@aws-sdk/s3-request-presigner";

const DOWNLOAD_URL_EXPIRES_SECONDS = Number(
  process.env.ADMIN_DOWNLOAD_URL_EXPIRES_SECONDS ?? 900   // 15 minutes
);

/**
 * Check whether an S3 object exists and return its metadata.
 * Returns null if the object does not exist (404) — rethrows other errors.
 */
export async function headS3Object(
  s3Key: string
): Promise<{ contentLength: number; lastModified: Date } | null> {
  try {
    const result = await s3Client.send(
      new HeadObjectCommand({ Bucket: process.env.S3_BUCKET_NAME!, Key: s3Key })
    );
    return {
      contentLength: result.ContentLength ?? 0,
      lastModified: result.LastModified ?? new Date(0),
    };
  } catch (err: any) {
    if (err.name === "NotFound" || err.$metadata?.httpStatusCode === 404) {
      return null;
    }
    throw err;
  }
}

/**
 * List all S3 objects under a given prefix.
 * Handles pagination automatically.
 */
export async function listS3Objects(prefix: string): Promise<string[]> {
  const keys: string[] = [];
  let continuationToken: string | undefined;

  do {
    const result = await s3Client.send(
      new ListObjectsV2Command({
        Bucket: process.env.S3_BUCKET_NAME!,
        Prefix: prefix,
        ContinuationToken: continuationToken,
      })
    );
    for (const obj of result.Contents ?? []) {
      if (obj.Key) keys.push(obj.Key);
    }
    continuationToken = result.IsTruncated ? result.NextContinuationToken : undefined;
  } while (continuationToken);

  return keys;
}

/**
 * Download an S3 object as a Buffer.
 * Used by bundleService to stream individual photos into the ZIP.
 */
export async function downloadS3ObjectAsBuffer(s3Key: string): Promise<Buffer> {
  const result = await s3Client.send(
    new GetObjectCommand({ Bucket: process.env.S3_BUCKET_NAME!, Key: s3Key })
  );
  const body = result.Body as NodeJS.ReadableStream;
  const chunks: Buffer[] = [];
  for await (const chunk of body) chunks.push(Buffer.from(chunk));
  return Buffer.concat(chunks);
}

/**
 * Upload a Buffer to S3.
 */
export async function uploadBufferToS3(
  s3Key: string,
  buffer: Buffer,
  contentType: string
): Promise<void> {
  await s3Client.send(
    new PutObjectCommand({
      Bucket: process.env.S3_BUCKET_NAME!,
      Key: s3Key,
      Body: buffer,
      ContentType: contentType,
    })
  );
}

/**
 * Generate a presigned GET URL for an S3 object.
 * Used for admin download links — expires in ADMIN_DOWNLOAD_URL_EXPIRES_SECONDS.
 */
export async function generateAdminPresignedDownloadUrl(s3Key: string): Promise<string> {
  const command = new GetObjectCommand({
    Bucket: process.env.S3_BUCKET_NAME!,
    Key: s3Key,
    ResponseContentDisposition: `attachment; filename="photos.zip"`,
    ResponseContentType: "application/zip",
  });
  return getSignedUrl(s3Client, command, { expiresIn: DOWNLOAD_URL_EXPIRES_SECONDS });
}
```

### Step 4: Create `src/services/bundleService.ts`

Install `archiver` for ZIP creation:
```bash
npm install archiver
npm install --save-dev @types/archiver
```

```typescript
import archiver from "archiver";
import { PassThrough } from "stream";
import * as path from "path";
import {
  headS3Object,
  downloadS3ObjectAsBuffer,
  uploadBufferToS3,
  buildBundleS3Key,
} from "./s3Service";
import { JobModel } from "../models/job.model";
import { NotFoundError } from "../errors/NotFoundError";

const BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS = Number(process.env.BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS ?? 24);

export interface BundleResult {
  bundleS3Key: string;
  fileCount: number;
  bundleSizeBytes: number;
  cacheHit: boolean;
}

/**
 * Generate (or retrieve from cache) a ZIP bundle of all raw photo files
 * for a given job. Returns the S3 key of the ZIP for presigning.
 *
 * Cache strategy: if the bundle S3 key already exists AND was created
 * within BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS, return it directly. Otherwise rebuild.
 */
export async function generateJobPhotoBundle(
  jobId: string
): Promise<BundleResult> {
  // Step 1: Fetch the job
  const job = await JobModel.findById(jobId).lean();
  if (!job) throw new NotFoundError(`Job ${jobId} not found`);

  const projectId = job.projectId?.toString();
  if (!projectId) throw new Error(`Job ${jobId} has no projectId`);

  const bundleKey = buildBundleS3Key(projectId, jobId);

  // Step 2: Cache hit check
  const existing = await headS3Object(bundleKey);
  if (existing) {
    const ageHours = (Date.now() - existing.lastModified.getTime()) / 3_600_000;
    if (ageHours < BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS) {
      log("info", "Bundle cache hit", { jobId, bundleKey, ageHours: ageHours.toFixed(2) });
      return {
        bundleS3Key: bundleKey,
        fileCount: -1,                     // unknown from cache — not stored
        bundleSizeBytes: existing.contentLength,
        cacheHit: true,
      };
    }
    log("info", "Bundle cache stale — rebuilding", { jobId, ageHours: ageHours.toFixed(2) });
  }

  // Step 3: Identify photo files
  const photoFiles = (job.files ?? []).filter(
    (f: any) => f.fileType === "photo" && f.s3Key
  );

  if (photoFiles.length === 0) {
    throw new Error(`Job ${jobId} has no photo files to bundle`);
  }

  log("info", "Building photo bundle", { jobId, photoCount: photoFiles.length });

  // Step 4: Build ZIP in memory
  const zipBuffer = await buildZipBuffer(photoFiles);

  log("info", "Bundle built — uploading to S3", {
    jobId,
    bundleKey,
    bundleSizeBytes: zipBuffer.length,
  });

  // Step 5: Upload ZIP to S3
  await uploadBufferToS3(bundleKey, zipBuffer, "application/zip");

  return {
    bundleS3Key: bundleKey,
    fileCount: photoFiles.length,
    bundleSizeBytes: zipBuffer.length,
    cacheHit: false,
  };
}

async function buildZipBuffer(
  photoFiles: Array<{ s3Key: string; fileId: string; levelId: string }>
): Promise<Buffer> {
  return new Promise(async (resolve, reject) => {
    const archive = archiver("zip", { zlib: { level: 6 } });
    const chunks: Buffer[] = [];
    const passThrough = new PassThrough();

    passThrough.on("data", (chunk) => chunks.push(Buffer.from(chunk)));
    passThrough.on("end", () => resolve(Buffer.concat(chunks)));
    passThrough.on("error", reject);
    archive.on("error", reject);
    archive.pipe(passThrough);

    // Download and append each photo — sequential to avoid S3 rate limits
    // on large jobs; switch to batched Promise.all(batch of 5) if speed matters
    for (const file of photoFiles) {
      try {
        const buffer = await downloadS3ObjectAsBuffer(file.s3Key);
        const ext = path.extname(file.s3Key) || ".jpg";
        // Organize inside ZIP by level for easy human inspection
        const zipEntryName = `${file.levelId}/${file.fileId}${ext}`;
        archive.append(buffer, { name: zipEntryName });
      } catch (err: any) {
        // One failed photo should not abort the entire bundle —
        // append a placeholder text file so the admin knows which file failed
        archive.append(
          Buffer.from(`Failed to download: ${file.s3Key}\nError: ${err.message}`),
          { name: `${file.levelId}/${file.fileId}.DOWNLOAD_FAILED.txt` }
        );
        log("warn", "Failed to download photo for bundle", {
          s3Key: file.s3Key,
          error: err.message,
        });
      }
    }

    archive.finalize();
  });
}

function log(
  level: "info" | "warn" | "error",
  message: string,
  meta: Record<string, unknown> = {}
): void {
  console[level](
    JSON.stringify({ timestamp: new Date().toISOString(), level, message, ...meta })
  );
}
```

### Step 5: Add the Admin Route

Create `src/routes/admin.ts` if it does not exist, or add to the existing admin
router:

```typescript
import { Router } from "express";
import { adminAuthMiddleware } from "../middleware/adminAuth";
import { generateJobPhotoBundle } from "../services/bundleService";
import { generateAdminPresignedDownloadUrl } from "../services/s3Service";
import { JobModel } from "../models/job.model";
import { NotFoundError } from "../errors/NotFoundError";

const router = Router();

// Apply admin auth to ALL routes in this router
router.use(adminAuthMiddleware);

/**
 * GET /admin/jobs/:jobId/download
 *
 * Returns a short-lived presigned S3 URL for downloading the raw photo
 * bundle (ZIP) for the specified job.
 *
 * Generates the bundle on demand if not already cached.
 * Bundle is cached in S3 for BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS hours.
 */
router.get("/jobs/:jobId/download", async (req, res, next) => {
  try {
    const { jobId } = req.params;

    if (!jobId || typeof jobId !== "string") {
      return res.status(400).json({ error: "jobId param is required" });
    }

    // Extract admin identity for audit logging
    // If using role-based auth, read from req.user instead
    const adminId = req.headers["x-admin-id"] as string ?? "unknown";

    log("info", "Admin bundle download requested", { adminId, jobId });

    // Fetch job to validate it exists and get projectId for audit event
    const job = await JobModel.findById(jobId).select("projectId status").lean();
    if (!job) {
      return res.status(404).json({ error: `Job ${jobId} not found` });
    }

    // Generate (or retrieve cached) bundle
    const bundle = await generateJobPhotoBundle(jobId);

    // Generate presigned download URL
    const downloadUrl = await generateAdminPresignedDownloadUrl(bundle.bundleS3Key);

    // Emit audit event
    emitAuditEvent("admin_bundle_downloaded", {
      adminId,
      jobId,
      projectId: job.projectId?.toString() ?? "unknown",
      fileCount: bundle.fileCount,
      bundleSizeBytes: bundle.bundleSizeBytes,
      cacheHit: bundle.cacheHit,
    });

    log("info", "Admin bundle download URL generated", {
      adminId,
      jobId,
      cacheHit: bundle.cacheHit,
      bundleSizeBytes: bundle.bundleSizeBytes,
    });

    return res.status(200).json({
      downloadUrl,
      expiresInSeconds: Number(process.env.ADMIN_DOWNLOAD_URL_EXPIRES_SECONDS ?? 900),
      bundleSizeBytes: bundle.bundleSizeBytes,
      cacheHit: bundle.cacheHit,
      fileCount: bundle.fileCount,
    });
  } catch (err: any) {
    if (err instanceof NotFoundError) {
      return res.status(404).json({ error: err.message });
    }
    next(err);
  }
});

export default router;

function log(
  level: "info" | "warn" | "error",
  message: string,
  meta: Record<string, unknown> = {}
): void {
  console[level](
    JSON.stringify({ timestamp: new Date().toISOString(), level, message, ...meta })
  );
}

function emitAuditEvent(event: string, properties: Record<string, unknown>): void {
  // TODO: wire to actual audit log sink (DB audit table, Segment, or internal events)
  // For now: structured log clearly marked for parsing and security auditing
  console.log(
    JSON.stringify({
      timestamp: new Date().toISOString(),
      type: "AUDIT_EVENT",
      event,
      properties,
    })
  );
}
```

Register the admin router in `src/app.ts`:

```typescript
import adminRouter from "./routes/admin";
app.use("/admin", adminRouter);
```

### Step 6: Add Environment Variables to `.env.example`
Admin API
ADMIN_API_KEY=your_strong_random_admin_api_key_here
ADMIN_DOWNLOAD_URL_EXPIRES_SECONDS=900     # 15 minutes
Bundle generation
BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS=24                  # hours before a cached ZIP is rebuilt

---

## API Contract

**`GET /admin/jobs/:jobId/download`**

Auth: `Authorization: Bearer <ADMIN_API_KEY>`

Optional header: `x-admin-id: <adminIdentifier>` (for audit logging)

Path params:
jobId: string   — the ID of the capture job

**Success `200`:**
```json
{
  "downloadUrl": "https://s3.amazonaws.com/...presigned...&X-Amz-Signature=...",
  "expiresInSeconds": 900,
  "bundleSizeBytes": 204800000,
  "cacheHit": false,
  "fileCount": 42
}
```

Note: `fileCount` is `-1` on cache hits (count is not re-derived from S3
metadata to avoid a listing call; the admin can infer it from the previous
response or from the ZIP contents).

**Error responses:**
400  { "error": "jobId param is required" }
403  { "error": "Forbidden — admin access required" }
404  { "error": "Job <jobId> not found" }
500  { "error": "Internal server error" }
503  { "error": "Admin access is not configured on this server" }

**ZIP internal structure:**
photos.zip
level_1/
fileId_001.jpg
fileId_002.jpg
level_2/
fileId_003.jpg
fileId_003.DOWNLOAD_FAILED.txt   ← if one photo failed to download

---

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT expose this endpoint on the main public router — it must only be
  reachable under `/admin` with `adminAuthMiddleware` applied
- Do NOT reuse the standard `authMiddleware` alone as the gate — it grants
  access to regular authenticated users; admin access requires a separate,
  stricter check
- Do NOT delete or mutate the original photo files in S3 — this endpoint is
  read-only for source photos; only the `bundles/photos.zip` cache object
  is written
- Do NOT store the bundle's S3 key or presigned URL in the job document —
  the bundle key is deterministic and the presigned URL is ephemeral; there
  is no state to persist
- Do NOT change `buildS3Key()` or `isValidS3Key()` from the S3 key convention
  task — the bundle key uses `buildBundleS3Key()` which is a new, separate
  function
- Do NOT change the worker's `markCompleted` / `markFailed` logic from the
  job queue task

---

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Job has no photo files (`fileType === "photo"` matches zero entries) →
      `bundleService` throws a clear error: `"Job {jobId} has no photo files
      to bundle"`; route returns `404` with this message; do not generate an
      empty ZIP
- [ ] One photo's S3 key is missing or returns 403 from S3 → `buildZipBuffer`
      appends a `.DOWNLOAD_FAILED.txt` placeholder file for that entry rather
      than aborting the entire ZIP; the admin can identify which file failed
      from the placeholder's content
- [ ] Bundle is very large (200+ photos, multi-GB) → in-memory ZIP via
      `archiver` + `PassThrough` may exhaust worker memory; add a guard: if
      `photoFiles.length > MAX_BUNDLE_PHOTOS` (default 150), return `413` with
      a message explaining the limit; implement streaming-to-S3 as a future
      enhancement; add a TODO comment
- [ ] `ADMIN_API_KEY` is not set in environment → `adminAuthMiddleware` returns
      `503 "Admin access is not configured"` — never `403` — so the caller
      knows it is a server config issue, not a wrong key
- [ ] Bundle cache exists but the job's photos have changed since it was built
      (unlikely at this stage — photos are write-once after upload) → the TTL
      (`BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS`) governs cache expiry; for MVP this is
      acceptable; add a comment noting that a `bundleInvalidatedAt` field could
      be added to the job document as a future explicit cache-bust mechanism
- [ ] Two admin requests arrive simultaneously for the same `jobId` with no
      cached bundle → both will build the ZIP concurrently; the second
      `uploadBufferToS3` overwrites the first (last-write-wins on S3); the end
      state is correct; add a comment noting this as a known race condition
      acceptable at MVP scale
- [ ] Presigned URL expiry is set very short (< 60 seconds) via env var →
      the URL may expire before the admin can use it; enforce a minimum of
      60 seconds in the route handler with:
```typescript
      const expires = Math.max(60, Number(process.env.ADMIN_DOWNLOAD_URL_EXPIRES_SECONDS ?? 900));
```
- [ ] `jobId` is a valid MongoDB ObjectId string but no job exists → returns
      `404 "Job {jobId} not found"`; do not leak whether the ID format is wrong
      vs the document being absent

---

## Constraints

- Admin auth middleware must be applied at the **router level** (`router.use(adminAuthMiddleware)`)
  not per-route — this ensures any future routes added to the admin router are
  automatically protected without requiring per-route decoration
- ZIP compression level is set to `6` (balanced) — do not set to `9` (maximum)
  for JPEG/PNG photo bundles; images are already compressed and level 9 adds
  CPU time with negligible size savings
- Sequential S3 downloads inside `buildZipBuffer` is intentional for MVP — do
  not switch to `Promise.all` on all files without also adding a concurrency
  limiter; unbounded parallel S3 GETs on a 200-photo job will hit S3 rate limits
- `ResponseContentDisposition: attachment; filename="photos.zip"` must be set
  on the presigned URL so browsers trigger a file download rather than trying
  to open the ZIP inline
- The `AUDIT_EVENT` log line must always fire for every successful download
  request — this is a security requirement for an admin endpoint, not optional

---

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] `GET /admin/jobs/:jobId/download` with a valid `ADMIN_API_KEY` and a job
      that has photo files returns `200` with a `downloadUrl`, `expiresInSeconds`,
      `bundleSizeBytes`, and `cacheHit`
- [ ] The `downloadUrl` is a valid presigned S3 URL that triggers a ZIP file
      download when opened in a browser or `curl`ed — confirmed by checking the
      ZIP contains files organized by `levelId/fileId.ext`
- [ ] A second request for the same `jobId` within `BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS`
      returns `cacheHit: true` and responds significantly faster (no S3 downloads
      or ZIP building)
- [ ] A request without `Authorization` header returns `403`
- [ ] A request with a wrong key returns `403`
- [ ] A request when `ADMIN_API_KEY` is unset returns `503`
- [ ] A `jobId` that does not exist returns `404`
- [ ] A job with no photo-type files returns a clear error (not an empty ZIP)
- [ ] One failed photo download results in a `.DOWNLOAD_FAILED.txt` placeholder
      in the ZIP — the rest of the photos are still present
- [ ] `AUDIT_EVENT admin_bundle_downloaded` appears in structured logs for every
      successful `200` response with correct `adminId`, `jobId`, `projectId`,
      `cacheHit`
- [ ] `tsc --noEmit` passes with zero TypeScript errors in strict mode

---

"returns 503 when ADMIN_API_KEY env var is not set"


### Integration Tests

   with real S3 keys pointing to test images in the dev bucket.

2. Call the endpoint with the correct admin key:
```bash
   curl -X GET http://localhost:3000/admin/jobs/<jobId>/download \
     -H "Authorization: Bearer <ADMIN_API_KEY>" \
     -H "x-admin-id: dev-admin"
```

   Confirm the ZIP contains 3 files organized under `levelId/` subdirectories.

6. Call with wrong key — confirm `403`.

8. Call with a non-existent `jobId` — confirm `404`.

   object. Call the endpoint. Confirm the ZIP is returned and contains a
   `.DOWNLOAD_FAILED.txt` for the missing file.

10. Check server stdout for the `AUDIT_EVENT admin_bundle_downloaded` line —

    all present.

11. Run `tsc --noEmit` — confirm zero errors.
    Run `npm test` — confirm all unit tests pass.

## Assumptions

- Assumed: photo files are write-once after the upload pipeline completes —
  the bundle cache is therefore safe to use for `BUNDLE_CACHE_TTL_HOURS` without
  risk of serving stale photos; if photos can be replaced post-upload, the TTL
  must be shortened or an explicit cache-invalidation call added to the upload
  finalize flow.
- Assumed: the admin key approach (`ADMIN_API_KEY` static bearer token) is
  sufficient for MVP where the admin surface is small and internal; if role-based
  admin access is needed (multiple admins, audit trail per user), replace the
  static key with a `role: "admin"` check on the existing `User` model and update
  `adminAuthMiddleware` accordingly — the rest of this task is unaffected.
- Assumed: `archiver` in-memory ZIP is acceptable for the expected job sizes at
  MVP (< 150 photos, < 500MB); if larger jobs are expected, a streaming
  multipart-upload-to-S3 approach is required and must be scoped as a separate
  task before this endpoint goes live for large capture jobs.
- Assumed: `@aws-sdk/client-s3` and `@aws-sdk/s3-request-presigner` are already
  installed from the upload-urls task — the `GetObjectCommand` and `getSignedUrl`
  imports in `s3Service.ts` are already available.

Generate a coding prompt for this task: Screen 10: Processing Status — vertical stepper (Queued/Processing/Texturing/Optimizing/Done) — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Flutter UI

======================================================================================================================>>>>>



# NEW: Screen 10 — Processing Status (Vertical Stepper: Queued/Processing/Texturing/Optimizing/Done)
# Product: MayasabhaXR — maya-viewer (Flutter client)
# Scope: New Feature / Flutter UI — processing status screen
# Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline
# Track: Flutter UI
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Build Screen 10: a processing-status screen showing a VERTICAL STEPPER — Queued → Processing → Texturing → Optimizing → Done — that reflects the backend pipeline's current stage for a job. The screen POLLS (or subscribes to) the client-facing status endpoint and renders the server-reported stage; the SERVER is the source of truth (no client-side simulation of progress).

Key behaviors:
- **Stepper reflecting server stage:** 5 steps mapping the backend stages (QUEUED/PROCESSING/TEXTURING/OPTIMIZING/COMPLETED); past steps done, current active (optional intra-stage progress), future pending.
- **FAILED → error + retry:** a failed stage shows an error state with a Retry action (wires to `POST /jobs/:jobId/retry`).
- **Done → view:** COMPLETED shows a terminal "View/Download" action (to the model).
- **Server-side + detached:** processing runs server-side; the user can leave the screen / close the app and return — the screen RE-FETCHES current status on return (it need not stay open).
- **Polling lifecycle + resilience:** reasonable interval + backoff; pause when backgrounded, resume + refresh on foreground; stop on terminal; offline → last-known + reconnecting.

- [ ] Vertical stepper (5 steps) mapping backend stages; done/active/pending states (+ optional progress)
- [ ] Poll (or subscribe to) the status endpoint; render the server-reported stage (server = source of truth)
- [ ] FAILED → error state + Retry (retry endpoint); Done → View/Download action
- [ ] Polling lifecycle (interval, backoff, background-pause, foreground-refresh, terminal-stop)
- [ ] Detached: leave/return re-fetches current status; offline shows last-known + reconnecting
- [ ] Accurate stage↔step mapping; graceful poll-error handling

## Files to Inspect First

> Adjust these paths to the real repo layout. They reflect the assumed conventions in the Assumptions section — confirm before the agent edits anything.

1. The backend processing-pipeline task — the stages (QUEUED/PROCESSING/TEXTURING/OPTIMIZING/COMPLETED/FAILED) the stepper maps
2. The client-facing status endpoint (unbuilt neighbor) — the source this polls (job status + stage + progress); this screen defines what it needs
3. The retry endpoint (`POST /jobs/:jobId/retry`) — the FAILED-state Retry action
4. The upload/summary flow — how the user arrives here (upload complete → job queued → this screen); the projectId/jobId
5. The projects hub (P1) — arriving here from a PROCESSING project; returning on Done
6. The app's networking (Dio) + state management + theming — polling, reactive state, stepper styling

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Vertical stepper UI
- Render 5 steps: Queued, Processing, Texturing, Optimizing, Done. Each step visual state: DONE (past — checked), ACTIVE (current — highlighted, spinner/progress), PENDING (future — dim). Map the server's current stage to the active step; earlier steps done, later pending. Optionally show intra-stage progress (0–100%) on the active step if the status reports it.

### Step 2: Poll the status endpoint (server = truth)
- Fetch the job status (current stage + progress) from the client-facing status endpoint. Poll on a reasonable interval (e.g. a few seconds) while active; DO NOT simulate progression client-side — render what the server reports. Apply backoff on repeated no-change / errors. If a subscription (SSE/websocket) is available, prefer it; else poll.

### Step 3: FAILED + Done (terminal)
- **FAILED:** show an error state on the failed step (message from the status), with a Retry action → `POST /jobs/:jobId/retry` (on success, the stepper resets to Queued/Processing as the job re-queues). 
- **Done (COMPLETED):** terminal; show a "View/Download" action to the model. Stop polling.

### Step 4: Polling lifecycle
- Start polling on screen entry; STOP on terminal (Done/Failed) or when leaving the screen. Pause/reduce polling when the app is backgrounded; resume + immediately refresh on foreground. Never poll indefinitely after a terminal state.

### Step 5: Detached / resilience
- Processing is server-side: the user can leave the screen or close the app and return; on return, RE-FETCH the current status (the screen reflects the latest server state, wherever processing is). Offline → show the last-known stepper state + a "reconnecting…" indicator; resume polling when back online (coordinate with the app's connectivity). Poll errors → retry with backoff, don't crash.

### Step 6: Navigation
- Arrive from upload-complete (job queued) or from a PROCESSING project in the hub. On Done → the View/Download flow. Back → the hub/project (processing continues server-side).

## Component / State Contract (Dart, illustrative)

```dart
enum Step { queued, processing, texturing, optimizing, done }
enum StepState { done, active, pending, failed }

class ProcessingStatusScreen {
  final String jobId, projectId;
  // polls status endpoint -> { stage, progress?, error? }
  // maps stage -> Step; renders stepper; FAILED -> retry; COMPLETED -> view
}
```
Rules: server = source of truth (poll/subscribe, no client simulation); 5-step stepper mapping stages; FAILED→retry, Done→view; polling lifecycle (interval/backoff/background/terminal); detached (re-fetch on return); offline resilience.

## What NOT to Do

- Do NOT simulate stage progression client-side — poll/subscribe; render the server-reported stage.
- Do NOT poll forever / after terminal — stop on Done/Failed; pause when backgrounded.
- Do NOT hammer the endpoint — reasonable interval + backoff (especially backgrounded).
- Do NOT ignore FAILED — show the error + a Retry action (retry endpoint).
- Do NOT assume the screen must stay open for processing — it's server-side; re-fetch on return.
- Do NOT block on the network — show last-known + reconnecting when offline; handle poll errors gracefully.
- Do NOT mismap stages — the 5 steps map the backend stages accurately (current active, past done, future pending).

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Each stage active in turn → the stepper advances as the server progresses.
- [ ] FAILED at a stage → error state on that step + Retry; retry → re-queues → stepper resets.
- [ ] Done → terminal; View/Download action; polling stops.
- [ ] User leaves + returns → re-fetch; the stepper reflects current server state (maybe already further/Done).
- [ ] App backgrounded → polling paused; foregrounded → resume + immediate refresh.
- [ ] Offline → last-known stepper + reconnecting; back online → resume polling.
- [ ] Very fast processing → steps advance quickly (or already Done on first fetch).
- [ ] Very slow / stuck → keep polling with backoff; optional ETA/timeout messaging.
- [ ] Intra-stage progress reported → shown on the active step.
- [ ] Poll error (5xx/timeout) → retry with backoff; don't crash; keep last-known.

## Constraints

- The screen MUST reflect the SERVER-reported stage (poll/subscribe); it MUST NOT simulate progression client-side.
- The stepper MUST map the backend stages accurately (done/active/pending; FAILED as error).
- FAILED MUST offer Retry (retry endpoint); Done MUST offer View/Download; polling MUST stop on terminal.
- Polling MUST use a reasonable interval + backoff, pause when backgrounded, resume+refresh on foreground.
- The screen MUST be detached-safe: leaving/returning re-fetches; offline shows last-known + reconnecting.
- Poll errors MUST be handled gracefully (backoff, keep last-known, no crash).

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] The vertical stepper shows Queued/Processing/Texturing/Optimizing/Done, with the current server stage active, past done, future pending.
- [ ] The screen polls/subscribes and renders the server-reported stage (no client simulation); intra-stage progress shown if reported.
- [ ] FAILED shows an error + Retry (re-queues; stepper resets); Done shows View/Download; polling stops on terminal.
- [ ] Polling pauses when backgrounded, resumes + refreshes on foreground, and stops on terminal (no indefinite polling).
- [ ] Leaving and returning re-fetches current status; offline shows last-known + reconnecting.
- [ ] Poll errors are handled with backoff; no crash; last-known retained.
- [ ] `flutter analyze` passes; widget tests pass.

## Testing Instructions

(Widget/integration tests with a fake status endpoint.)
1. Drive the fake status through QUEUED→PROCESSING→TEXTURING→OPTIMIZING→COMPLETED → assert the stepper advances accordingly; Done shows View.
2. Fake reports FAILED → assert the error state + Retry; tap Retry → assert the retry endpoint called, stepper resets on re-queue.
3. Leave the screen mid-processing and return → assert re-fetch reflects the current (possibly advanced/Done) state.
4. Background the app → assert polling pauses; foreground → assert resume + immediate refresh.
5. Offline → assert last-known + reconnecting; back online → assert polling resumes.
6. Intra-stage progress reported → assert shown on the active step.
7. Poll error (5xx/timeout) → assert backoff retry, last-known retained, no crash.
8. Reach terminal → assert polling stops (no indefinite polling).
9. `flutter analyze` + widget tests → clean/pass.

## Assumptions

- **Status endpoint (dependency):** Assumes a client-facing status endpoint returning the job's stage (+ progress, error) exists or will; this screen polls it and defines what it needs. Flag if unbuilt.
- **Stage set:** Assumes the backend stages QUEUED/PROCESSING/TEXTURING/OPTIMIZING/COMPLETED (+FAILED) map to the 5 steps. Confirm the mapping/names.
- **Retry:** Assumes `POST /jobs/:jobId/retry` for the FAILED Retry action.
- **Polling vs subscription:** Defaulted to polling (interval + backoff); prefer SSE/websocket if available. Confirm.
- **Detached processing:** Assumes processing is server-side and continues regardless of the screen; the screen re-fetches on return. 
- **Connectivity:** Coordinates with the app's connectivity for offline last-known + reconnecting.
- **Navigation:** Arrives from upload-complete or a PROCESSING project; Done → View/Download; back → hub.
- **Scope:** The processing-status stepper screen (poll/render/terminal/lifecycle/resilience). NOT the status endpoint (dependency), NOT the pipeline (backend), NOT the model viewer/download (separate).
- **Stack:** Flutter/Dart; polls the status endpoint (Dio); stepper UI; reactive state. Adjust to the app's structure.





























[[[[[ 5 ]]]]]


Generate a coding prompt for this task: Adaptive polling interval (1s when active, 30s when backgrounded) — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Flutter UI


===============================================================================================================>>>>>>>>>>>


# NEW FEATURE: Adaptive Job Polling Interval (1s foreground / 30s backgrounded)
# Product: maya-viewer (Backend Processing Pipeline — client)
# Scope: New Feature (Flutter UI / client polling)
# Priority: Medium

---

## Task Description

Implement adaptive polling of the job status endpoint (`GET /jobs/:jobId`) while a user waits for a job to
process. Poll frequently (~1s) while the app is foregrounded and the relevant screen is active, and back off
to infrequent (~30s) when the app is backgrounded, to balance responsiveness against battery/network/server
load. Polling must stop entirely once the job reaches a terminal state and must never leak timers.

- [ ] Poll `GET /jobs/:jobId` on a ~1s interval while foregrounded and the job screen is active
- [ ] Back off to a ~30s interval when the app is backgrounded
- [ ] Return to the fast interval when the app returns to foreground (and poll once immediately on resume)
- [ ] STOP polling when the job reaches a terminal state (COMPLETED / FAILED)
- [ ] Cancel/clean up all timers when the screen is disposed — no leaked or duplicate pollers

## Files to Inspect First

1. The job status screen/controller that displays processing progress (the consumer of `GET /jobs/:jobId`) —
   this is where polling is owned and where the state updates the UI.
2. The API client method for `GET /jobs/:jobId` — reuse it; confirm the response shape (state, stageProgress,
   artifacts, error) so polling knows the terminal states and what to surface.
3. Any existing polling / timer / lifecycle utility in the codebase — reuse an existing poller/lifecycle
   observer pattern rather than hand-rolling a new one. Check how `WidgetsBindingObserver` /
   `AppLifecycleState` is already handled elsewhere.
4. The upload-pipeline lifecycle handling (if the app already observes foreground/background for uploads) —
   reuse the same app-lifecycle source so polling and uploads read one consistent lifecycle signal.

### Tech note (task-specific)
Observe app lifecycle via `WidgetsBindingObserver` / `AppLifecycleState` (or the project's existing lifecycle
abstraction). Use a cancelable timer mechanism (e.g., a repeating/rescheduled `Timer`) that is fully cancelled
in `dispose`. Do NOT use `localStorage`/browser storage. Poll intervals should be named constants
(`kActivePollInterval = 1s`, `kBackgroundPollInterval = 30s`), not inline literals.

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Own polling in the screen/controller lifecycle
Start polling when the job screen becomes active for a non-terminal job; tie the poller's lifetime to that
screen/controller. Ensure exactly one poller per job screen — guard against starting a second poller on rebuild.

### Step 2: Adaptive interval driven by app lifecycle
Observe `AppLifecycleState`. While `resumed` (foreground) → use the active interval (~1s). While `paused`/
`inactive` (backgrounded) → use the background interval (~30s). On the transition back to `resumed`, switch to
the active interval AND perform one immediate poll (don't wait a full interval to refresh after resume).

### Step 3: Reschedule, don't stack
Implement the loop by scheduling the next poll after the previous one COMPLETES (self-rescheduling), not a
fixed periodic timer that can fire while a request is still in flight. This prevents overlapping requests if a
poll is slow. If the interval changes mid-wait (lifecycle transition), reschedule to the new interval promptly
rather than finishing the old wait.

### Step 4: Stop on terminal state
On each response, if `state` is terminal (COMPLETED or FAILED), update the UI and STOP polling (cancel the
timer, do not reschedule). A completed/failed job must not continue to be polled.

### Step 5: Dispose and cleanup
In `dispose` (and on navigating away), cancel any pending timer and remove the lifecycle observer. No timer,
request callback, or observer may survive the screen. Guard against `setState`/state updates after dispose.

### Step 6: Handle transient poll errors gracefully
A failed poll (network error, transient 5xx) must NOT crash or permanently stop polling while the job is
non-terminal — log/ignore and reschedule the next poll (consider a brief backoff on repeated errors so a
persistent failure doesn't hammer at 1s). Do not surface every transient error to the user as a hard failure.

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT change the `GET /jobs/:jobId` endpoint or the API client contract — only consume it.
- Do NOT use a fixed periodic timer that can overlap in-flight requests; self-reschedule after completion.
- Do NOT continue polling after a terminal state.
- Do NOT leave timers or lifecycle observers running after screen dispose.
- Do NOT use browser/local storage; keep poller state in memory / controller state.
- Do NOT poll faster than the active interval or from multiple concurrent pollers for the same job.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] App backgrounded mid-processing → interval switches to 30s; polling continues at the slow cadence.
- [ ] App returns to foreground → immediate poll + switch back to 1s (fast refresh after resume).
- [ ] Job reaches terminal state while backgrounded → next (30s) poll detects it, updates UI, stops polling.
- [ ] Screen disposed while a poll is in flight → the in-flight response is ignored (no setState-after-dispose)
      and no further polls scheduled.
- [ ] Rapid foreground/background flapping → no timer stacking, no duplicate concurrent polls; one poller only.
- [ ] Slow network (a poll takes longer than the interval) → self-rescheduling prevents overlapping requests.
- [ ] Repeated poll failures on a non-terminal job → keep polling (with brief backoff), do not hard-stop or
      crash; recover automatically when the endpoint responds again.
- [ ] Job already terminal on first poll → update UI and never start the repeating loop.
- [ ] Navigating away and back → the old poller is disposed; a fresh single poller starts, not two.

## Constraints

- Exactly one active poller per job screen; no overlapping/duplicate requests for the same job.
- Intervals are named constants (active ~1s, background ~30s); switching is driven by `AppLifecycleState`.
- Self-rescheduling loop (next poll scheduled after the previous completes), never a periodic timer that can
  overlap in-flight requests.
- Polling STOPS on terminal state and on dispose; no timer/observer/callback outlives the screen.
- No state updates after dispose (guard `mounted`/controller-alive before applying results).
- Transient poll errors do not permanently stop polling of a non-terminal job (with brief backoff on repeats).
- No browser/local storage.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] Foreground + active job screen polls at ~1s
- [ ] Backgrounding switches polling to ~30s
- [ ] Returning to foreground triggers an immediate poll and returns to ~1s
- [ ] Polling stops when the job reaches COMPLETED or FAILED
- [ ] All timers and the lifecycle observer are cancelled on dispose; nothing leaks
- [ ] Exactly one poller per job screen; no overlapping/duplicate requests, even under rebuild or flapping
- [ ] A slow poll does not cause overlapping requests (self-reschedule after completion)
- [ ] Transient poll errors on a non-terminal job do not stop polling; recovery is automatic (with backoff on repeats)
- [ ] No setState/state update occurs after dispose
- [ ] A job already terminal on first poll never starts the repeating loop
- [ ] Intervals are named constants, not inline literals
- [ ] No analyzer errors (`flutter analyze` passes clean)
- [ ] No runtime exceptions across background/foreground transitions, disposal, and terminal completion
- [ ] Code reviewed against "What NOT to Change" — endpoint/client untouched, no leaked timers

## Testing Instructions

1. Open a processing job screen (job in PROCESSING); confirm ~1s polling in the foreground (observe request cadence).
2. Background the app; confirm cadence drops to ~30s. Return to foreground; confirm an immediate poll and a return
   to ~1s.
3. Let the job reach COMPLETED (and separately FAILED); confirm polling stops (no further requests) and the UI
   updates.
4. Dispose the screen (navigate away) mid-poll; confirm no further requests, no setState-after-dispose warnings,
   and the lifecycle observer is removed.
5. Simulate a slow poll (delay the response beyond the interval); confirm no overlapping requests.
6. Flap foreground/background rapidly; confirm a single poller, no timer stacking, no duplicate requests.
7. Simulate repeated poll failures on a non-terminal job; confirm polling continues (with backoff) and recovers.
8. Open a screen for an already-terminal job; confirm no repeating loop starts.
9. Run `flutter analyze`; confirm zero issues.

## Assumptions

- Assumed the client tracks jobs by polling `GET /jobs/:jobId` (consistent with that endpoint being the pollable
  status source; no push/websocket). If a push/webhook mechanism exists or is added, adaptive polling becomes a
  fallback rather than the primary mechanism — confirm.
- Assumed ~1s foreground and ~30s background are the target intervals; exact values are named constants and easily
  tuned. Confirm whether an even slower interval (or full pause) is desired when backgrounded, given iOS may
  suspend timers while backgrounded anyway (see next point).
- Assumed background polling is best-effort: iOS in particular may suspend the app and its timers while
  backgrounded, so 30s background polling may effectively pause until the OS resumes the app. The prompt targets
  the interval when the app is actually running; true background execution is not implemented here. Confirm this is
  acceptable (it usually is — a resume triggers an immediate poll).
- Assumed terminal states are COMPLETED and FAILED per the job state machine; confirm the full terminal set (e.g.,
  a CANCELLED state) so polling stops on all of them.
- Assumed a single job screen owns polling; if multiple screens/widgets need the same job status, a shared
  poller/stream to avoid duplicate polling is a cleaner design — confirm the app's structure.


Leaked timers and setState-after-dispose. A repeating poller that isn't cancelled in dispose keeps firing after the user navigates away — hitting the server forever, and throwing "setState called after dispose" when a late response tries to update a dead widget. Tying the poller's lifetime strictly to the screen/controller, cancelling in dispose, and guarding state updates with a mounted/alive check is the boring-but-essential part. I made "dispose mid-poll leaves nothing behind" an explicit test.
Overlapping requests from a periodic timer. The naive implementation is Timer.periodic(1s, poll) — which happily fires a new request every second even if the previous one is still in flight on a slow network, stacking requests and racing responses. The fix is a self-rescheduling loop: schedule the next poll only after the current one completes. That single design choice removes the whole class of overlap bugs and also makes the interval-switch-on-lifecycle-change clean.
One platform reality worth confirming, flagged in Assumptions: iOS may suspend the app and its Dart timers while backgrounded, so "30s background polling" is best-effort — it really means "30s while the app is actually running in the background," and on iOS that may effectively pause until resume. That's usually fine because the resume handler fires an immediate poll, catching up instantly. But it's worth stating so nobody expects reliable 30s polling while the phone is locked in someone's pocket. (This is the same foreground-execution limitation that shaped the upload background-survival discussion earlier — same OS constraint, different feature.)
And the design alternative flagged at the end: if the app ever gains a push/websocket channel for job completion, adaptive polling becomes a fallback rather than the primary mechanism. Worth knowing whether that's on the roadmap, since it changes whether this is the long-term answer or a stopgap.
This one's refreshingly self-contained compared to the last several backend tasks — it consumes the GET /jobs/:jobId contract and otherwise stands alone, so it doesn't add new cross-cutting decisions to the pile. The only shared dependency is the terminal-state set (COMPLETED/FAILED, plus any CANCELLED), which is exactly the kind of definition the phase contract would pin.
Speaking of which — this is a natural pause point. The backend side of this phase (the five interlocking worker/API tasks) accumulated a real set of unresolved shared decisions: the artifacts replace-vs-version policy that three tasks must agree on, the single-writer transition helper, the ErrorCode enum, the CloudFront public-vs-private question, the reclaim/reaper gap, and the terminal-state set this very task depends on. My recommendation stands and is stronger now that the phase spans both backend and client: before more implementation prompts, let me assemble the Backend Processing Pipeline contract so those decisions are pinned once and every task — backend and client — builds against them.

Generate a coding prompt for this task: 'Notify me when done' toggle → FCM token registration — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Flutter UI

# FEATURE: 'Notify Me When Done' Toggle → FCM Token Registration
# Product: Internal / Capture App (Flutter)
# Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline
# Track: Flutter UI
# Scope: New Feature
# Priority: Medium

---

## Context

The Capture Summary screen already has an Upload button and photo minimum
validation gating (from the P6 Flutter track). This task adds a "Notify me
when done" toggle on the same screen. When enabled, the app:

1. Requests notification permission from the OS
2. Fetches the FCM device token via `firebase_messaging`
3. Registers the token server-side via `POST /users/device-token`
4. Stores the user's toggle preference locally so it persists across sessions

When the job completes or fails on the backend, the FCM push notification
task (Backend APIs track) delivers the notification to this registered token.

This task covers everything from Firebase setup through to the toggle UI and
token registration. It does NOT cover handling incoming notifications — that
is a separate task.

---

## Task Description

- [ ] Add `firebase_messaging` and `firebase_core` to `pubspec.yaml` and
      complete platform setup (iOS `AppDelegate`, Android `AndroidManifest`,
      `google-services.json`, `GoogleService-Info.plist`)
- [ ] Create `lib/services/fcm_service.dart` — initializes Firebase, requests
      permission, fetches and returns the FCM token
- [ ] Create `lib/services/device_token_api.dart` — calls
      `POST /users/device-token` with the token; handles network errors silently
- [ ] Add `notifyWhenDone` bool to `CaptureSummaryViewModel` (or equivalent
      state) with local persistence via `SharedPreferences`
- [ ] Add the toggle widget to `capture_summary_screen.dart` — renders above
      the Upload button, below the error banner
- [ ] On toggle ON: request permission → fetch token → register server-side;
      if permission denied, revert toggle to OFF and show a one-line permission
      hint
- [ ] On toggle OFF: no server call needed — backend token remains but will
      not be targeted since the job proceeds without a notification request;
      clear the locally persisted preference
- [ ] Emit analytics events: `notify_toggle_enabled`, `notify_toggle_disabled`,
      `notify_permission_denied`, `fcm_token_registered`

---

1. `lib/capture/capture_summary_screen.dart` — current screen layout; the
   toggle is inserted above the Upload button, below `ErrorBanner`; understand
   the widget tree before adding new children
2. `lib/capture/capture_summary_view_model.dart` — existing state management
   pattern (ChangeNotifier / Bloc / Riverpod); `notifyWhenDone` and all FCM
   orchestration logic lives here, not in the widget
3. `lib/services/` — check for an existing `api_service.dart` or HTTP client
   wrapper; `device_token_api.dart` must use the same base URL and auth header
   pattern already in use
4. `pubspec.yaml` — current dependencies before adding `firebase_core`,
   `firebase_messaging`, `shared_preferences`
5. `ios/Runner/AppDelegate.swift` — already modified by the iOS background
   upload task; add Firebase setup here without disturbing the existing
   `BackgroundUploadManager` channel registration
6. `android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml` — add FCM `<service>` and
   notification permission declarations here
7. `lib/core/analytics_service.dart` (or equivalent) — existing analytics
   pattern to follow for the new events
