### Step 4: Create `lib/services/fcm_service.dart`

```dart
import 'package:firebase_messaging/firebase_messaging.dart';

enum FcmPermissionStatus { granted, denied, notDetermined }

class FcmService {
  static final FcmService _instance = FcmService._internal();
  factory FcmService() => _instance;
  FcmService._internal();

  final FirebaseMessaging _messaging = FirebaseMessaging.instance;

  /// Request notification permission from the OS.
  /// Returns the resulting permission status.
  Future<FcmPermissionStatus> requestPermission() async {
    final settings = await _messaging.requestPermission(
      alert: true,
      badge: true,
      sound: true,
      provisional: false,
    );

    switch (settings.authorizationStatus) {
      case AuthorizationStatus.authorized:
      case AuthorizationStatus.provisional:
        return FcmPermissionStatus.granted;
      case AuthorizationStatus.denied:
        return FcmPermissionStatus.denied;
      case AuthorizationStatus.notDetermined:
        return FcmPermissionStatus.notDetermined;
    }
  }

  /// Check current permission status without prompting.
  Future<FcmPermissionStatus> checkPermission() async {
    final settings = await _messaging.getNotificationSettings();
    switch (settings.authorizationStatus) {
      case AuthorizationStatus.authorized:
      case AuthorizationStatus.provisional:
        return FcmPermissionStatus.granted;
      case AuthorizationStatus.denied:
        return FcmPermissionStatus.denied;
      default:
        return FcmPermissionStatus.notDetermined;
    }
  }

  /// Fetch the FCM device token.
  /// Returns null if the token cannot be retrieved (offline, permission denied).
  Future<String?> getToken() async {
    try {
      return await _messaging.getToken();
    } catch (e) {
      debugPrint('[FcmService] Failed to get token: $e');
      return null;
    }
  }
}
```

### Step 5: Create `lib/services/device_token_api.dart`

```dart
import 'dart:convert';
import 'package:http/http.dart' as http;

class DeviceTokenApi {
  static final DeviceTokenApi _instance = DeviceTokenApi._internal();
  factory DeviceTokenApi() => _instance;
  DeviceTokenApi._internal();

  /// Register or refresh the FCM token server-side.
  /// Fails silently — a failed registration means no push notification,
  /// but the upload and job processing are unaffected.
  Future<bool> registerToken(String token, String authToken) async {
    try {
      final response = await http
          .post(
            Uri.parse('${ApiConfig.baseUrl}/users/device-token'),
            headers: {
              'Content-Type': 'application/json',
              'Authorization': 'Bearer $authToken',
            },
            body: jsonEncode({'token': token}),
          )
          .timeout(const Duration(seconds: 10));

      if (response.statusCode == 200) {
        debugPrint('[DeviceTokenApi] Token registered successfully');
        return true;
      } else {
        debugPrint('[DeviceTokenApi] Registration failed: ${response.statusCode} ${response.body}');
        return false;
      }
    } catch (e) {
      debugPrint('[DeviceTokenApi] Registration error: $e');
      return false;
    }
  }
}
```

Replace `ApiConfig.baseUrl` with whatever base URL pattern is already used in
the project. Use the existing HTTP client (Dio, http package, etc.) if one is
already configured — do not introduce a second HTTP client.

### Step 6: Add State to `CaptureSummaryViewModel`

Add the following to the view model. If the view model uses Bloc or Riverpod,
adapt the pattern without changing the logic.

```dart
import 'package:shared_preferences/shared_preferences.dart';
import '../services/fcm_service.dart';
import '../services/device_token_api.dart';

// Preference key — stable string, do not rename after shipping
static const _kNotifyWhenDoneKey = 'capture_notify_when_done';

bool _notifyWhenDone = false;
bool get notifyWhenDone => _notifyWhenDone;

bool _isRegisteringToken = false;
bool get isRegisteringToken => _isRegisteringToken;

// One-line hint shown below the toggle when permission is denied
String? _permissionHint;
String? get permissionHint => _permissionHint;

/// Called once on screen init to restore persisted toggle state.
Future<void> loadNotifyPreference() async {
  final prefs = await SharedPreferences.getInstance();
  _notifyWhenDone = prefs.getBool(_kNotifyWhenDoneKey) ?? false;
  notifyListeners();
}

/// Called when the user taps the toggle.
Future<void> setNotifyWhenDone(bool value, String authToken) async {
  if (value == _notifyWhenDone) return;

  if (!value) {
    // Toggle OFF — clear preference, no server call needed
    _notifyWhenDone = false;
    _permissionHint = null;
    final prefs = await SharedPreferences.getInstance();
    await prefs.setBool(_kNotifyWhenDoneKey, false);
    analytics.track('notify_toggle_disabled', {
      'jobId': jobId,
      'projectId': projectId,
    });
    notifyListeners();
    return;
  }

  // Toggle ON — request permission, fetch token, register
  _isRegisteringToken = true;
  _permissionHint = null;
  notifyListeners();

  try {
    final fcm = FcmService();
    final permissionStatus = await fcm.requestPermission();

    if (permissionStatus == FcmPermissionStatus.denied) {
      // Revert toggle — user denied permission
      _notifyWhenDone = false;
      _isRegisteringToken = false;
      _permissionHint =
          'Enable notifications in Settings to use this feature.';
      analytics.track('notify_permission_denied', {
        'jobId': jobId,
        'projectId': projectId,
        'platform': Platform.operatingSystem,
      });
      notifyListeners();
      return;
    }

    final token = await fcm.getToken();
    if (token == null) {
      // Token fetch failed — revert toggle silently
      _notifyWhenDone = false;
      _isRegisteringToken = false;
      _permissionHint = 'Could not register for notifications. Try again.';
      notifyListeners();
      return;
    }

    final api = DeviceTokenApi();
    final registered = await api.registerToken(token, authToken);

    _notifyWhenDone = true;
    _isRegisteringToken = false;
    _permissionHint = null;

    final prefs = await SharedPreferences.getInstance();
    await prefs.setBool(_kNotifyWhenDoneKey, true);

    analytics.track('notify_toggle_enabled', {
      'jobId': jobId,
      'projectId': projectId,
      'platform': Platform.operatingSystem,
      'tokenRegistered': registered,
    });

    if (registered) {
      analytics.track('fcm_token_registered', {
        'jobId': jobId,
        'projectId': projectId,
        'platform': Platform.operatingSystem,
      });
    }
  } catch (e) {
    debugPrint('[CaptureSummaryViewModel] Notification setup error: $e');
    _notifyWhenDone = false;
    _isRegisteringToken = false;
    _permissionHint = 'Something went wrong. Try again.';
  } finally {
    _isRegisteringToken = false;
    notifyListeners();
  }
}
```

Call `loadNotifyPreference()` from `initState` (or equivalent lifecycle hook)
of `capture_summary_screen.dart`.

### Step 7: Add Toggle Widget to `capture_summary_screen.dart`

Insert the toggle block **between** the `ErrorBanner` (photo deficit error)
and the Upload button. Do not change the order of existing widgets.

```dart
// Notification toggle block
_NotifyToggle(
  value: viewModel.notifyWhenDone,
  isLoading: viewModel.isRegisteringToken,
  permissionHint: viewModel.permissionHint,
  onChanged: (value) => viewModel.setNotifyWhenDone(
    value,
    context.read<AuthState>().authToken,  // adjust to actual auth token accessor
  ),
),
```

Create as a private widget in the same file:

```dart
class _NotifyToggle extends StatelessWidget {
  final bool value;
  final bool isLoading;
  final String? permissionHint;
  final ValueChanged<bool> onChanged;

  const _NotifyToggle({
    required this.value,
    required this.isLoading,
    required this.permissionHint,
    required this.onChanged,
  });

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Column(
      crossAxisAlignment: CrossAxisAlignment.start,
      children: [
        Row(
          mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.spaceBetween,
          children: [
            const Text(
              'Notify me when done',
              style: TextStyle(fontSize: 15, fontWeight: FontWeight.w500),
            ),
            isLoading
                ? const SizedBox(
                    width: 24,
                    height: 24,
                    child: CircularProgressIndicator(strokeWidth: 2),
                  )
                : Switch(
                    value: value,
                    onChanged: isLoading ? null : onChanged,
                  ),
          ],
        ),
        if (permissionHint != null)
          Padding(
            padding: const EdgeInsets.only(top: 4),
            child: Text(
              permissionHint!,
              style: TextStyle(
                fontSize: 12,
                color: Colors.red.shade400,
              ),
            ),
          ),
      ],
    );
  }
}
```

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT change the Upload button's enabled/disabled logic — it remains driven
  purely by `hasPhotoDeficit`; the toggle state has no effect on the Upload
  button
- Do NOT move FCM token fetching into `main.dart` or a global app init —
  token registration is intentionally deferred to when the user opts in, not
  at app launch
- Do NOT call `POST /users/device-token` on toggle OFF — the backend retains
  the token but sends no notification unless the job explicitly targets it;
  token deletion is a future enhancement
- Do NOT change `AppDelegate.swift`'s existing method channel or background
  upload session setup — only prepend `FirebaseApp.configure()` as the first
  line of `application(_:didFinishLaunchingWithOptions:)`
- Do NOT persist `notifyWhenDone = true` to `SharedPreferences` until the
  server registration call returns — optimistically saving then failing leaves
  the UI out of sync with reality

---

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] User toggles ON, grants permission, but FCM token fetch returns `null`
      (device offline, Play Services unavailable on some Android devices) →
      toggle reverts to OFF; `permissionHint` shows "Could not register for
      notifications. Try again."; no crash
- [ ] User toggles ON on iOS 14 or earlier where `requestPermission` behaves
      differently → `firebase_messaging` handles this internally; `provisional`
      status is treated as `granted` — confirm behaviorally on an older
      simulator
- [ ] `POST /users/device-token` returns non-200 (server error, expired auth
      token) → `registered` is `false`; toggle still flips to ON locally and
      `notifyWhenDone` is persisted; the user still sees the toggle as enabled;
      log the failure; the backend will simply have no token to notify — this
      is a degraded-but-acceptable state for MVP; do not revert the toggle on
      API failure to avoid confusing UX
- [ ] User denies permission on Android 13+ (runtime `POST_NOTIFICATIONS`
      permission) → `requestPermission()` resolves with `denied`; toggle
      reverts; hint shown; subsequent toggle ON taps call `requestPermission()`
      again — Android will not re-show the system dialog after two denials;
      detect this with `checkPermission()` on subsequent taps and show a
      settings-redirect hint instead:
```dart
      'Go to Settings → Notifications → enable for this app.'
```
- [ ] User toggles ON rapidly multiple times before the first registration
      completes → `isRegisteringToken` is `true` during registration; the
      `Switch` is disabled (`onChanged: isLoading ? null : onChanged`) — no
      double-registration
- [ ] Screen is popped and re-pushed while token registration is in flight →
      `loadNotifyPreference()` runs again on re-init; `_isRegisteringToken`
      resets to `false`; the in-flight registration continues in the background
      but its result is discarded since the view model may be replaced; this is
      acceptable at MVP
- [ ] `SharedPreferences` read fails on `loadNotifyPreference()` → default to
      `false` (toggle OFF); catch the exception, log it, and continue — do not
      crash on a preference read failure
- [ ] Firebase is not configured (missing `google-services.json` or
      `GoogleService-Info.plist`) → `Firebase.initializeApp()` throws at app
      startup; this surfaces as a crash immediately in development — treat it
      as a hard build-time error that must be fixed before shipping, not a
      runtime edge case; add a build-time check note in the CI setup

---

## Constraints

- FCM token registration is fire-and-forget after the toggle is set — never
  block the Upload button on token registration success or failure
- `setNotifyWhenDone` must never throw — all errors are caught internally and
  surface as a `permissionHint` string; the view model must remain in a
  consistent state regardless of what fails
- The `Switch` widget must be disabled (not just ignored) while `isLoading` is
  true — use `onChanged: isLoading ? null : onChanged`, not a boolean guard
  inside `onChanged`
- `loadNotifyPreference()` must be called on screen init (not lazily on first
  toggle interaction) so the toggle shows the correct persisted state
  immediately when the screen opens
- Do not request notification permission proactively at app launch — only
  request it when the user explicitly taps the toggle ON; premature permission
  requests are a common cause of user denial

---

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] Toggle renders on the Capture Summary screen between the `ErrorBanner`
      and the Upload button
- [ ] Toggle defaults to OFF on first use; restores its persisted state on
      subsequent screen opens
- [ ] Tapping toggle ON on a device with notifications enabled: permission
      dialog appears (first time), token is fetched, `POST /users/device-token`
      is called, toggle stays ON, no error hint shown
- [ ] Tapping toggle ON when permission is denied: toggle reverts to OFF,
      permission hint appears in red below the toggle
- [ ] A loading spinner replaces the Switch during token fetch + API call;
      Switch reappears after completion
- [ ] Toggle ON/OFF state has no effect on the Upload button's enabled state
- [ ] `notify_toggle_enabled`, `notify_toggle_disabled`, `notify_permission_denied`,
      `fcm_token_registered` analytics events appear in the debug console with
      correct properties
- [ ] `flutter analyze` reports zero errors or warnings introduced by this task
- [ ] App does not crash on cold launch — `Firebase.initializeApp()` completes
      before `runApp`
- [ ] Verified on physical Android device (API 33+) and physical iOS device

## Testing Instructions

### Functional Testing

1. Cold launch the app — confirm no crash, `Firebase.initializeApp()` completes.
2. Navigate to Capture Summary screen. Confirm toggle is OFF (first use).
3. Tap toggle ON. Confirm permission dialog appears (iOS/Android 13+).
4. Grant permission. Confirm loading spinner appears, then toggle shows ON,
   no hint shown.
5. Check server-side: `db.users.findOne({}, { deviceToken: 1 })` — confirm
   token is stored.
6. Kill and relaunch the app. Navigate to Capture Summary. Confirm toggle
   restores to ON.
7. Tap toggle OFF. Confirm toggle shows OFF, no API call made (check network
   proxy — no outbound request to `/users/device-token`).
8. Kill and relaunch. Confirm toggle restores to OFF.

### Permission Denied Testing

9. On Android: go to Settings → App → Notifications → Disable.
   Return to app, tap toggle ON. Confirm toggle reverts to OFF and hint
   "Enable notifications in Settings to use this feature." appears.

    behavior as step 9.

### Network Failure Testing

    permission dialog may not appear offline on Android).
    Confirm token fetch fails gracefully, toggle reverts to OFF, hint shown.

### Analytics Validation

    correct `jobId`, `projectId`, and `platform` properties.

    required (only needed API 33+).
15. Physical Android (API 33+) — confirm runtime permission dialog appears.
16. Physical iOS — confirm permission dialog appears on first toggle ON.

    spinner renders.

### Static Analysis

18. Run `flutter analyze` — confirm zero issues.
19. Run `flutter build apk --debug` and `flutter build ios --debug` (no
    codesign) — confirm clean builds with no Firebase configuration errors.

## Assumptions

- Assumed: an `authToken` is accessible from the widget tree (via `Provider`,
  `Riverpod`, `Bloc`, or equivalent) to pass into `setNotifyWhenDone` for the
  `Authorization` header on `POST /users/device-token` — adjust the auth token
  accessor to match whatever pattern is already used in the app.

  already configured with base URL and interceptors, replace the raw `http.post`

Generate a coding prompt for this task: 'Back to Projects' — processing continues in background — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Flutter UI


==================================================================================================================>>>>>>>>>>>



# NEW: "Back to Projects" — Processing Continues in the Background
# Product: MayasabhaXR — maya-viewer (Flutter client)
# Scope: New Feature / Flutter UI — navigation affordance
# Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline
# Track: Flutter UI
# Priority: Medium

---

## Task Description

Add a "Back to Projects" action on the processing-status screen (Screen 10) that returns the user to the projects hub while the reconstruction CONTINUES server-side. Leaving does NOT cancel or pause the job — only the client's polling stops; the server keeps processing. The hub reflects the project as PROCESSING, and the user can return to the status screen anytime.

This embodies the detached-processing model: processing is server-side and independent of the screen.

Key behaviors:
- **Back to Projects:** navigates to the projects hub.
- **Processing continues:** the server-side job is unaffected (NOT cancelled/paused); only the client screen/polling stops.
- **Communicated:** make clear processing continues in the background (a note; the hub shows PROCESSING).
- **Return path:** the PROCESSING project in the hub reopens Screen 10, which re-fetches current status (possibly advanced/Done).
- **Kill-safe:** even if the app is closed, processing continues server-side; on relaunch the hub reflects the true status.

- [ ] "Back to Projects" action on Screen 10 → navigate to the hub
- [ ] Leaving does NOT cancel/pause the job (client polling stops; server continues) — no cancel call
- [ ] Communicate "processing continues in the background"; the hub shows the project as PROCESSING
- [ ] Return path: PROCESSING project → Screen 10 (re-fetch current status)
- [ ] Handle system back / gesture the same way; kill-safe (server continues)
- [ ] (Optional/related) pair with a completion notification when done while away

## Files to Inspect First

> Adjust these paths to the real repo layout. They reflect the assumed conventions in the Assumptions section — confirm before the agent edits anything.

1. Screen 10 (processing-status stepper) task — the screen this action lives on; its polling lifecycle (client polling stops on leave)
2. The projects hub (P1) — the destination; how a PROCESSING project renders + is tapped to return
3. The project status model / completion-update task — `status = PROCESSING`/COMPLETED reflected in the hub
4. The client-facing status endpoint (dependency) — re-fetched on return
5. The app's navigation conventions — back stack, returning to the hub
6. (Optional) the notification/foreground-service tasks — a completion notification when done while away

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: The action
- Add a "Back to Projects" button/affordance on Screen 10. On tap → navigate to the projects hub (per the app's navigation; typically clearing back to the hub rather than stacking).

### Step 2: Processing continues (no cancel)
- Leaving MUST NOT call cancel/pause on the job — the server-side processing is independent. Only the CLIENT stops polling (Screen 10's lifecycle stops on leave). Do NOT conflate "stop polling" with "stop the job." No cancel/abort request is sent.

### Step 3: Communicate it
- Make clear processing continues in the background — e.g. a brief note near the action ("Processing will continue in the background; you can leave this screen"), and the project appearing as PROCESSING in the hub. The user should feel confident they aren't losing progress.

### Step 4: Hub reflects PROCESSING + return path
- In the hub, the project shows a PROCESSING status/indicator. Tapping it reopens Screen 10, which RE-FETCHES the current status (may be further along or Done). The return is seamless — the server state is the truth.

### Step 5: System back + kill-safe
- Handle the system back button / back gesture the same as the action (leave, processing continues). Kill-safe: if the app is closed entirely, processing continues server-side; on relaunch, the hub reflects the true status (PROCESSING/COMPLETED/FAILED).

### Step 6: (Optional/related) completion notification
- Optionally, when processing completes while the user is away, surface a notification (ties to the foreground-service/notification work). Flag as related but separate; coordinate if in scope.

## What NOT to Do

- Do NOT cancel/pause the server-side job on leave — only the client polling stops; the job continues (no cancel request).
- Do NOT conflate stopping the client's polling with stopping the job.
- Do NOT lose the return path — the hub shows PROCESSING; tapping returns to Screen 10 (re-fetch).
- Do NOT leave the user uncertain — communicate that processing continues in the background.
- Do NOT block leaving on anything — it's a simple navigation; processing is independent.
- Do NOT require the screen to stay open for processing — it's server-side/detached.
- Do NOT treat system back differently from the action.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Leave via Back to Projects → job continues; hub shows PROCESSING; no cancel sent.
- [ ] Return to Screen 10 → re-fetch current status (possibly advanced/Done since leaving).
- [ ] Processing completes while away → the project shows COMPLETED in the hub (+ optional notification).
- [ ] Leave then app killed → processing continues server-side; relaunch → hub reflects true status.
- [ ] System back / gesture → same as the action (leave, continue).
- [ ] Multiple processing projects → each shows its own status in the hub.
- [ ] Offline while leaving → navigation still works; status re-fetches when back online.
- [ ] FAILED while away → the project shows FAILED in the hub (with the retry path via Screen 10).

## Constraints

- "Back to Projects" MUST navigate to the hub WITHOUT cancelling/pausing the server-side job (no cancel request; only client polling stops).
- The UI MUST communicate that processing continues in the background; the hub MUST reflect the project as PROCESSING.
- The return path MUST work: PROCESSING project → Screen 10 → re-fetch current status.
- System back/gesture MUST behave the same; the flow MUST be kill-safe (server continues; relaunch reflects true status).
- Leaving MUST NOT require the screen to remain open (detached processing).

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] Tapping "Back to Projects" returns to the hub; no cancel/pause is sent; the server-side job continues.
- [ ] The UI communicates that processing continues in the background; the hub shows the project as PROCESSING.
- [ ] Tapping the PROCESSING project reopens Screen 10 and re-fetches current status (advanced/Done reflected).
- [ ] App-kill while away is safe — processing continues; relaunch reflects the true status.
- [ ] System back/gesture behaves identically; offline leaving still navigates (status re-fetches when online).
- [ ] `flutter analyze` passes; widget tests pass.

## Testing Instructions

1. On Screen 10 (processing), tap Back to Projects → assert navigation to the hub; assert NO cancel/pause request; the job continues (fake backend still processing).

6. Processing completes while away → assert the hub shows COMPLETED (+ optional notification if in scope).
7. Offline while leaving → assert navigation works; status re-fetches when back online.
8. `flutter analyze` + widget tests → clean/pass.

## Assumptions

- **Detached processing:** Assumes processing is server-side and independent of the screen (established by Screen 10). This action embodies it. 
- **Screen 10 + hub:** Assumes Screen 10 (status stepper) and the P1 projects hub exist; this wires the navigation + return path.
- **Project status:** Assumes the hub reflects the project status (PROCESSING/COMPLETED/FAILED) via the completion-update/status data. 
- **No cancel:** Leaving sends NO cancel/pause; only the client polling stops (Screen 10 lifecycle). Confirm there's no accidental teardown that cancels.
- **Completion notification:** Optional/related (foreground-service/notification tasks); coordinate if in scope. Confirm.
- **Navigation:** Assumes the app's hub navigation (clear-to-hub vs stack); follow the convention.
- **Scope:** The Back-to-Projects affordance + the continues-in-background guarantee/communication + return path. NOT the status screen itself (prior), NOT the pipeline (backend), NOT the notification system (optional/separate).
- **Stack:** Flutter/Dart; navigation from Screen 10 to the hub; project status in the hub. Adjust to the app's structure.

Generate a coding prompt for this task: Screen 10F: Processing failed — Re-capture / Retry processing / Contact support buttons — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Flutter UI


==================================================================================================================>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>



# NEW FEATURE: Screen 10F — Processing Failed (Re-capture / Retry / Contact Support)
# Product: maya-viewer (Backend Processing Pipeline — client)
# Scope: New Feature (Flutter UI)
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Build the Processing Failed screen (Screen 10F), shown when a job reaches the FAILED state. It communicates
the failure to the user with a clear, non-technical message and offers recovery actions: Re-capture, Retry
processing, and Contact support. Which actions are appropriate depends on the failure's `error.code`, so the
screen adapts its options to the failure reason rather than always showing all three identically.

- [ ] Render a clear, user-friendly failure message derived from the job's `error` (code + message)
- [ ] Offer recovery actions: Re-capture, Retry processing, Contact support
- [ ] Adapt which actions are primary/available based on `error.code` (see Action Mapping)
- [ ] Wire each action to its destination (re-capture flow / retry request / support channel)
- [ ] Do NOT expose raw internal error detail, codes-as-jargon, or stack traces to the user

## Files to Inspect First

1. The job status screen/controller and adaptive poller (the flow that lands here on FAILED) — confirm how 10F
   is reached and what job/error data is available when it is (the `GET /jobs/:jobId` response: state, error.code, error.message).
2. The `GET /jobs/:jobId` response shape and the `ErrorCode` enum (from the failure task) — confirm the set of
   codes so the screen can map them to user messaging and appropriate actions.
3. The capture flow entry point (Screen 5-series / capture start) — the Re-capture action routes back into a new
   capture for the same project; confirm how a fresh capture is started.
4. The retry mechanism — determine whether the client has a user-facing retry path. If retry means re-triggering
   processing, note that the admin rerun endpoint is admin-only; a user-facing retry needs its own endpoint or
   a different mechanism (see Assumptions). Confirm what "Retry processing" actually calls.
5. The support/contact mechanism — find the existing support channel (in-app form, email intent, help URL) to wire
   Contact support to; reuse it rather than inventing one.
6. Existing button / screen / message widgets — reuse the design-system components and error/empty-state patterns.
7. `lib/services/analytics_service.dart` (or equivalent) — match the existing event-logging signature.

## Action Mapping (error.code → recommended actions)

Confirm against the actual `ErrorCode` set; this is the intended logic:
- Input/capture-quality failures (e.g., FILE_COUNT_MISMATCH, MANIFEST_INVALID, insufficient/poor coverage) →
  RE-CAPTURE is the primary action (retrying processing on bad input won't help). Retry may be hidden/secondary.
- Transient/server processing failures (e.g., PROCESSING_ERROR, INTERNAL_ERROR) → RETRY PROCESSING is primary
  (the input is fine; reprocessing may succeed). Re-capture secondary.
- Unknown/unmapped codes → show all actions with Contact support prominent (safe default).
- Contact support is ALWAYS available regardless of code.

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Consume the failure data
Read the job's `state == FAILED` and its `error.{code, message}` from the status data passed into / fetched by
this screen. Do not re-poll here unless that's the screen's data source; consume what the flow provides.

### Step 2: Render user-friendly messaging
Show a clear headline and a friendly explanation. Prefer a mapped, human message per `error.code` (so users see
"We couldn't process your capture — the photos didn't cover the object fully" rather than a raw code). Use the
server `error.message` only if it is confirmed client-safe; never render internal detail, codes as-is, or stack
traces. Fall back to a generic friendly message for unmapped codes.

### Step 3: Render adaptive actions
Based on `error.code` (Action Mapping), determine which action is primary and which are secondary/hidden. Render
the buttons using design-system components. Contact support is always present. Do not present Retry as primary for
input-quality failures where it cannot help.

### Step 4: Wire the actions
- Re-capture → route into a fresh capture flow for the same project (confirm whether it starts a brand-new job or
  reuses the project context).
- Retry processing → call the user-facing retry mechanism (endpoint TBD — see Assumptions); on success, route to
  the processing/polling screen (10-series) so the user watches the retried job. Guard against double-tap.
- Contact support → open the existing support channel, ideally pre-filled with non-sensitive context (jobId,
  error.code) to help support — confirm what context is safe to include.

### Step 5: Handle action outcomes and states
Show appropriate feedback: Retry that fails to enqueue shows a non-blocking error and keeps the user on 10F;
Re-capture navigates away cleanly. Guard against double navigation / duplicate retry calls.

## Analytics Events

Event: `processing_failed_view`
Trigger: when Screen 10F is shown
Properties: capture_session_id / job_id, error_code:string, device_type:"mobile"

Event: `processing_failed_action`
Trigger: when the user taps a recovery action
Properties: capture_session_id / job_id, error_code:string, action:"recapture"|"retry"|"contact_support", device_type

Match the existing event-logging signature and the id used across the job funnel so 10F joins it. `error_code`
here is fine for analytics (internal), but must not be shown raw to the user in the UI.

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT expose raw `error.code`, internal `error.detail`, or stack traces in the UI — user-facing copy only.
- Do NOT present Retry as the primary/only fix for input-quality failures where reprocessing can't help.
- Do NOT implement or change the backend retry/rerun endpoint here — this screen calls whatever the confirmed
  user-facing retry mechanism is.
- Do NOT add new theme tokens or one-off buttons; reuse the design system.
- Do NOT change the failure/status endpoints or the `ErrorCode` enum.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] `error` present but `code` unmapped/unknown → generic friendly message; all actions shown; support prominent.
- [ ] `error` missing entirely on a FAILED job (invariant violation) → show a generic failure message and support,
      do not crash or show a blank screen.
- [ ] Retry action enqueue fails (network/endpoint error) → non-blocking error, stay on 10F, allow re-try of the tap.
- [ ] Rapid double-tap on Retry → only one retry request/navigation (guard).
- [ ] Re-capture tapped → navigates cleanly into capture; back-navigation doesn't strand the user on a dead 10F.
- [ ] Input-quality failure → Retry hidden or clearly secondary; Re-capture primary (the mapping actually changes
      the UI, verified).
- [ ] Support channel unavailable (e.g., no network for an in-app form) → degrade gracefully (e.g., show support
      email/URL) rather than a hard failure.
- [ ] Small screen (720p low-end Android) → message + up to three buttons fit without overflow; scroll-safe.

## Constraints

- User-facing copy only; no raw codes, internal detail, or stack traces surfaced.
- Recovery actions ADAPT to `error.code` per the Action Mapping; the mapping must be a single, testable place, not
  scattered conditionals.
- Contact support is always available.
- Retry and Re-capture navigations are guarded against double-tap/double-navigation.
- Reuse design-system components and the existing support channel; no one-off UI.
- Layout is scroll-safe on a 720p low-end Android device.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] Screen 10F renders on FAILED with a clear, user-friendly message (no raw code/detail/stack)
- [ ] Recovery actions adapt to `error.code`: input-quality → Re-capture primary; transient/server → Retry primary
- [ ] Contact support is always available
- [ ] Re-capture routes into a fresh capture flow for the project
- [ ] Retry processing calls the confirmed user-facing retry mechanism and, on success, routes to the polling screen
- [ ] Contact support opens the existing channel with non-sensitive context (jobId, error_code) if supported
- [ ] Unknown/missing error code → generic friendly message with all actions + prominent support, no crash
- [ ] Retry enqueue failure keeps the user on 10F with a non-blocking error; double-tap guarded
- [ ] `processing_failed_view` and `processing_failed_action` fire with correct `error_code`/`action`
- [ ] Layout scroll-safe on a 720p low-end Android device
- [ ] No analyzer errors (`flutter analyze` passes clean)
- [ ] No runtime exceptions across mapped/unmapped codes, missing error, and each action path
- [ ] Code reviewed against "What NOT to Change" — no raw error exposure, no backend endpoint changes

## Testing Instructions

1. Drive a job to FAILED with an input-quality code (e.g., FILE_COUNT_MISMATCH); open 10F and confirm the message
   is friendly and Re-capture is primary (Retry hidden/secondary).
2. Drive a job to FAILED with a transient/server code (e.g., PROCESSING_ERROR); confirm Retry is primary.
3. Confirm no raw `error.code`, `detail`, or stack text appears anywhere in the UI.
4. Tap Re-capture; confirm it routes into a fresh capture flow cleanly.
5. Tap Retry (with a working retry mechanism); confirm it triggers reprocessing and routes to the polling screen;
   double-tap and confirm one request/navigation.
6. Make the retry call fail; confirm a non-blocking error and that the user stays on 10F.
7. Tap Contact support; confirm the existing channel opens with safe context.
8. Open 10F with an unmapped code and with a missing `error`; confirm generic messaging + support, no crash.
9. Confirm analytics fire with correct `error_code` and `action`.
10. Test layout on a 720p low-end Android emulator; confirm no overflow.
11. Run `flutter analyze`; confirm zero issues.

## Assumptions

- Assumed 10F is reached from the job polling/status flow when `state == FAILED`, with `error.{code, message}`
  available from `GET /jobs/:jobId`. The `code` drives adaptive actions; the user-facing message is a mapped,
  friendly string (not the raw code).
- KEY UNKNOWN — the "Retry processing" mechanism: the admin rerun endpoint (`POST /admin/jobs/:jobId/rerun`) is
  ADMIN-ONLY, so a regular user cannot call it. A user-facing retry needs either (a) a dedicated user-facing
  retry endpoint, or (b) retry implemented as a fresh job from the existing uploaded bundle, or (c) Retry simply
  routing back to re-upload/re-finalize. This must be confirmed before wiring Retry — the button cannot call the
  admin endpoint. If no user-facing retry exists yet, Retry may need to be deferred/hidden or a backend task
  scoped first.
- Assumed the `error.code` → action mapping above; confirm against the actual `ErrorCode` set and which codes are
  input-quality (Re-capture) vs transient (Retry). The mapping should live in one place shared with any messaging table.
- Assumed the user-facing message is either a client-side mapping keyed by `error.code` or a confirmed-client-safe
  server `error.message`. Confirm which is authoritative so support and app show consistent language.
- Assumed Contact support uses an existing channel (in-app form / email / help URL); reuse it. Confirm what context
  is safe to attach (jobId and error_code are typically fine; no PII/secrets).
- Assumed Re-capture starts a fresh capture for the same project; confirm whether it creates a new job or reuses
  project context, and how it relates to the failed job (abandon vs link).

Generate a coding prompt for this task: Job versioning: re-capture creates new job version, history shown in overflow menu — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Flutter UI


================================================================================================================>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>



# NEW FEATURE: Job Versioning — Re-capture Creates a New Version, History in Overflow Menu
# Product: maya-viewer (Backend Processing Pipeline)
# Scope: New Feature (Flutter UI)
# Priority: High

---

## Task Description

Surface **job versioning** in the UI for a project. Each capture→upload→process cycle is a **job version**. **Re-capturing creates a new version** (it never overwrites or replaces a prior one), and the user can view the **version history** from the screen's **overflow (⋮) menu**, selecting any version to view it.

The defining invariant: versioning is **non-destructive**. Re-capture always adds a new version; the UI offers no path that overwrites or deletes an earlier version. The UI consumes the backend's job-version data (list, status, current version); it initiates re-capture and reflects the resulting new version, but does not implement the backend versioning itself.

- [ ] Add a **Version history** entry to the screen's overflow (⋮) menu.
- [ ] Show a history view listing all job versions for the project, newest first, each with a version label, timestamp, status, and a current-version indicator.
- [ ] Selecting a version views that version's result/detail (or switches the displayed version).
- [ ] Wire **Re-capture** to start a capture cycle that creates a **new** job version; reflect the new version in history without overwriting prior ones.
- [ ] Update version statuses reactively as they process (queued → processing → succeeded/failed).
- [ ] Fire analytics for opening history, selecting a version, and starting a re-capture.

## Files to Inspect First

1. The **project / result / processing screen** where the overflow (⋮) menu lives (or should be added) — the host for the Version history entry and Re-capture action.
2. The **job/version data model + repository** — how to list a project's versions, read each version's number/timestamp/status, and identify the **current** version. This is the source of the history; it must not be hardcoded.
3. The **re-capture entry point** — how a re-capture is initiated and how it maps to **creating a new job version** on the backend. Confirm the API/flow that produces a new version (vs any existing update path).
4. The **processing status mechanism** — how a version's status updates (stream/poll), so the history reflects live state.
5. The **navigation / router** — how to view a specific version's result/detail.
6. Existing **overflow-menu, list-item, status-badge, and bottom-sheet/dialog components** and **theme / design tokens** — consume them; do not invent new styles.
7. The **analytics service**.

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Overflow menu entry
Add a **Version history** item to the existing overflow (⋮) menu on the host screen, using the app's standard menu component. If Re-capture is part of this screen's actions, add/confirm it here too. Do not restyle the menu.

### Step 2: Version history view
Open a history surface (bottom sheet, dialog, or screen — match the app's pattern) that lists the project's job versions from the repository:
- **Newest first** (deterministic order by creation/version number).
- Each row: version label (e.g. `v1`, `v2`, by creation order), timestamp, a **status** badge (queued / processing / succeeded / failed), and a clear **current** indicator on the active version.
- Derive everything from the version data — do not hardcode the count or labels.
- Handle a single-version project (just `v1`) and many versions (scrolls) gracefully.

### Step 3: Select a version to view
Tapping a version navigates to that version's result/detail (or switches the displayed version, per the screen's model), carrying the version id. The current/displayed version is clearly indicated. Viewing a processing or failed version shows the appropriate state (in-progress / failure), consistent with how the processing pipeline surfaces those states elsewhere.

### Step 4: Re-capture creates a new version
Wire the Re-capture action to start the capture cycle (reusing the existing guided-capture flow) in a mode that results in a **new job version** for the project via the backend versioning API:
- The new version is added; **all prior versions remain** and stay viewable in history.
- The UI must not present any "overwrite," "replace," or "delete previous" option as part of re-capture.
- Once the new version is created/enqueued, it appears in history (typically as the newest, processing) and becomes the current version per the backend's rule.
Confirm the intended behavior when a re-capture is started while another version is still processing (allow a new queued version, or warn/block) — see Assumptions.

### Step 5: Reactive status
Subscribe to the version status mechanism so history rows and the current indicator update as versions move through queued → processing → succeeded/failed, without requiring a manual refresh.

### Step 6: Analytics
Fire the events below.

## Analytics Events

Match existing conventions.

Event: `job_version_history_opened`
Trigger: when the Version history view is opened from the overflow menu.
Properties: `project_id` (string), `version_count` (number)

Event: `job_version_selected`
Trigger: when the user taps a version to view it.
Properties: `project_id` (string), `version` (string/number), `status` (string)

Event: `job_recapture_started`
Trigger: when a re-capture is initiated (new version creation begins).
Properties: `project_id` (string), `previous_version_count` (number)

## What NOT to Change

This section is mandatory.

- Do NOT implement or change the backend job-versioning logic, the version data schema, or the processing pipeline. Consume the existing model/APIs.
- Do NOT modify the guided-capture flow itself; re-capture reuses it.
- Do NOT provide any UI path that overwrites, replaces, or deletes a prior version — versioning is non-destructive.
- Do NOT hardcode the version list, labels, or count — derive from the version data.
- Do NOT introduce new menu, list, badge, or sheet styles outside the design system.
- Do NOT change the upload pipeline or gating from prior tasks; a re-captured version flows through the same capture→upload→process path.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Single version (`v1`) → history opens and shows one row with the current indicator; no crash on a one-item list.
- [ ] Many versions → list scrolls; ordering is stable newest-first.
- [ ] A version still processing → shown with a processing status; selecting it shows the in-progress state, not a broken/empty result.
- [ ] A failed version → shown with a failed status; selecting it surfaces the failure state (per the pipeline's failure presentation).
- [ ] Re-capture started while another version is processing → behavior per the confirmed rule (new queued version, or warn); the UI stays coherent and shows both.
- [ ] Version data unavailable / not yet loaded → loading state; no crash; no empty-but-final flash.
- [ ] Offline → history renders from cached version data if available; re-capture requires the normal capture flow and network per the pipeline.
- [ ] Current-version indicator stays correct after a new version becomes current.

## Constraints

Task-specific only.

- Versioning is non-destructive in the UI: re-capture only adds; no overwrite/delete path is offered.
- The history is derived from the version data (labels, order, status, current) — never hardcoded.
- Ordering is deterministic (newest first by version/creation).
- The view is reactive to status changes; no manual refresh needed to see a version progress.
- Re-capture reuses the existing capture→upload→process path and the backend versioning API.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] The overflow (⋮) menu exposes Version history on the host screen.
- [ ] The history view lists all job versions newest-first with version label, timestamp, status badge, and a current-version indicator, all derived from the version data.
- [ ] Tapping a version views it (or switches the displayed version) with the correct version id; the current/displayed version is indicated.
- [ ] Re-capture starts a cycle that creates a **new** version; all prior versions remain and stay viewable; no overwrite/delete path exists in the UI.

## Testing Instructions

Generate a coding prompt for this task: Analytics: processing_started, processing_stage_changed, processing_completed, processing_failed — Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline, Track: Analytics / QA

=============================================================================================>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>>



# NEW: Analytics — processing_started / processing_stage_changed / processing_completed / processing_failed
# Product: MayasabhaXR — backend (Node + TypeScript)
# Scope: New Feature / Analytics (server-side emission)
# Phase: Backend Processing Pipeline
# Track: Analytics / QA
# Priority: Medium

---

## Task Description

Emit backend analytics for the processing pipeline as it transitions through stages — the server-side counterpart to the client capture/upload analytics, completing the funnel (capture → upload → process → done):
- **processing_started:** job enters PROCESSING (picked up / begins an attempt).
- **processing_stage_changed:** each subsequent stage transition (from/to), with per-stage duration.
- **processing_completed:** job reaches COMPLETED (with total duration, output summary).
- **processing_failed:** job → FAILED (with the failed stage + error).

Discipline (mirrors the client analytics, server-side):
- **Emit on genuine transitions** (not per-poll/passive).
- **Observe the state machine externally** — keep the pure pipeline logic analytics-free (observer/hook pattern).
- **Fire-and-forget** — analytics MUST NOT block or fail the pipeline; swallow errors.
- **PII posture** — hash ids where appropriate; no raw PII beyond what's needed (server context has userId).
- **Idempotent emission** — a crash-resume mid-attempt does NOT re-emit already-emitted events; a RETRY is a new attempt that legitimately emits `processing_started` again WITH the attempt number.

- [ ] Four events emitted on pipeline transitions (started/stage_changed/completed/failed)
- [ ] Observe the state machine externally; pipeline logic stays analytics-free
- [ ] Payloads: jobId, projectId, (hashed) userId, stage(s), attempt, timing/duration, error (failed), output summary (completed)
- [ ] Fire-and-forget (non-blocking, error-swallowing); PII posture applied
- [ ] Idempotent: crash-resume doesn't duplicate; retry emits with the attempt number
- [ ] Consistent with the canonical analytics conventions + the client funnel; lands in the backend sink

## Files to Inspect First

> Adjust these paths to the real repo layout. They reflect the assumed conventions in the Assumptions section — confirm before the agent edits anything.

1. The processing-pipeline state-machine task — the transitions (QUEUED→PROCESSING→…→COMPLETED/FAILED) to observe; the hook/observer points; attempts
2. The idempotent-job-processing task — the emit-once semantics (crash-resume no re-emit; retry = new attempt); analytics as an idempotent side effect
3. The client analytics tasks (canonical AnalyticsEvent layer, PII guardrail, fire-and-forget, observer pattern) — the conventions to mirror server-side + the funnel to complement
4. The backend telemetry/analytics sink — where server events go (metrics/events pipeline, warehouse, service); the emit API
5. The job model — jobId/projectId/userId, stage timestamps, attempts, error, outputRefs for payloads
6. Any server-side PII/hashing utility — for hashed ids

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Observe transitions externally
- Hook the pipeline's stage transitions (from the state machine) via an observer/emitter — do NOT embed analytics calls in the pure pipeline logic. On each genuine transition, the observer emits the corresponding event.

### Step 2: The four events + payloads
- **processing_started:** on entering PROCESSING (attempt begins). Payload: jobId, projectId, hashed userId, attempt, startedAt, (device/uploadSize if useful).
- **processing_stage_changed:** on each transition (PROCESSING→TEXTURING→OPTIMIZING…). Payload: from/to stage, attempt, the completed stage's duration, ts.
- **processing_completed:** on COMPLETED. Payload: total duration, per-stage durations (if tracked), output summary (counts/sizes), attempt, ts.
- **processing_failed:** on FAILED. Payload: failed stage, error code/message, attempt, duration-so-far, ts.

### Step 3: Fire-and-forget + PII
- Emit non-blocking; NEVER block or fail the pipeline on analytics (swallow errors, no throw). Apply the PII posture: hash userId (and other ids as appropriate); include no raw PII/image data beyond what's needed. Server context legitimately includes userId (hashed) for internal analytics — be deliberate.

### Step 4: Idempotent emission (KEY)
- Emit ONCE per genuine transition. On a crash-resume within the SAME attempt, do NOT re-emit events already emitted for that attempt (track emitted markers, or emit only on real state transitions the resume doesn't repeat). 
- A RETRY is a NEW attempt: it legitimately emits `processing_started` again, tagged with the incremented `attempt`, so analytics can distinguish attempts. Terminal events (`completed`/`failed`) emit once per attempt's terminal outcome.

### Step 5: Consistency + sink
- Name/shape the events consistently with the canonical analytics conventions (adapted server-side) so they complement the client funnel (capture → upload → processing → done). Emit to the backend telemetry sink. Payloads conform to the sink's/warehouse's schema.

## Event Payloads (illustrative)

```
processing_started:       { jobId, projectId, userIdHash, attempt, startedAt, uploadSize?, device? }
processing_stage_changed: { jobId, projectId, userIdHash, attempt, fromStage, toStage, stageDurationMs, ts }
processing_completed:     { jobId, projectId, userIdHash, attempt, totalDurationMs, stageDurations?, output: {counts,sizes}, ts }
processing_failed:        { jobId, projectId, userIdHash, attempt, failedStage, errorCode, errorMessage, durationSoFarMs, ts }
```
Rules: emit on genuine transitions; observe externally (pipeline analytics-free); fire-and-forget; PII posture (hashed ids); emit-once per attempt-transition (crash-resume no re-emit; retry = new attempt with attempt number); canonical/consistent; backend sink.

## What NOT to Do

- Do NOT emit per-poll/passive — only on genuine stage transitions.
- Do NOT embed analytics in the pure pipeline logic — observe transitions externally.
- Do NOT block or fail the pipeline on analytics — fire-and-forget; swallow errors.
- Do NOT double-emit on idempotent re-runs — crash-resume within an attempt doesn't re-emit; retry = new attempt emits with the attempt number.
- Do NOT include raw PII/image data beyond what's needed — hashed ids; deliberate server-side posture.
- Do NOT diverge from the canonical conventions — consistent naming/schema; complement the client funnel.
- Do NOT emit terminal events more than once per attempt's outcome.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Normal flow → started → stage_changed×N → completed (each once, correct durations).
- [ ] Failure → started → …→ failed (failed stage + error).
- [ ] Retry → a new attempt → processing_started again with incremented `attempt`; distinguishable from the first.
- [ ] Crash-resume mid-attempt → already-emitted events for that attempt NOT re-emitted.
- [ ] Very fast processing → events still fire per transition (possibly rapid).
- [ ] Stage durations → computed from stage timestamps; accurate.
- [ ] Analytics sink down → fire-and-forget; pipeline unaffected; no throw.
- [ ] Concurrent jobs → each emits its own events with its own ids/attempt.
- [ ] Duplicate queue delivery (idempotent no-op processing) → no duplicate events.

## Constraints

- The four events MUST emit on genuine pipeline transitions, observed EXTERNALLY (pipeline logic analytics-free).
- Emission MUST be fire-and-forget (non-blocking, error-swallowing) — never blocks/fails the pipeline.
- Emission MUST be idempotent: crash-resume within an attempt does not re-emit; a retry emits as a new attempt (with `attempt`); terminal events once per attempt outcome.
- Payloads MUST apply the PII posture (hashed ids; no raw PII beyond need) and conform to the backend sink schema.
- Events MUST be consistent with the canonical conventions and complement the client funnel.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] processing_started/stage_changed/completed/failed emit on the correct transitions with correct payloads (durations, attempt, error, output summary).
- [ ] Events are observed externally; the pure pipeline logic contains no analytics calls.
- [ ] Emission is fire-and-forget — a failing/absent sink never blocks or fails the pipeline.
- [ ] Idempotent: crash-resume doesn't duplicate events; a retry emits processing_started with the incremented attempt; terminal events fire once per attempt.
- [ ] Payloads apply the PII posture and conform to the backend sink schema; events complement the client funnel.
- [ ] Concurrent jobs and duplicate deliveries produce correct, non-duplicated events.
- [ ] Typecheck + tests pass.

## Testing Instructions

(Unit/integration tests with a fake sink + fake pipeline transitions.)
1. Run a job to completion → assert started → stage_changed×N → completed, each once, with correct durations/attempt/output.
2. Fail a stage → assert processing_failed with failed stage + error; prior events emitted once.
3. Retry the failed job → assert a new attempt emits processing_started with the incremented `attempt`, distinguishable.
4. Crash-resume mid-attempt → assert already-emitted events for that attempt are NOT re-emitted.
5. Sink throws/absent → assert the pipeline is unaffected (fire-and-forget); no throw.
6. Confirm no analytics calls in the pure pipeline logic (observed externally).
7. Inspect payloads → hashed ids, no raw PII, schema conformance, correct attempt/stage/timing.
8. Concurrent jobs + duplicate delivery → assert correct, non-duplicated events.
9. Typecheck + tests → clean/pass.

## Assumptions

- **Observes the state machine:** Assumes the pipeline exposes transition hooks/an emitter; analytics observes externally. Blocked without the state machine.
- **Idempotency + attempts:** Assumes the idempotent-processing semantics + an `attempt` counter; emit-once per attempt-transition; retry = new attempt. Confirm the attempt model.
- **Backend sink:** Assumes a server-side telemetry/analytics sink (metrics pipeline/warehouse/service); events conform to its schema. Confirm the sink + schema.
- **PII posture:** Assumes hashed ids (incl. userId) and no raw PII beyond need, consistent with the client guardrail adapted server-side. Confirm.
- **Canonical consistency:** Assumes the client's canonical event conventions; these complement the funnel (capture → upload → processing → done). Align naming/schema.
- **Fire-and-forget:** Non-blocking, error-swallowing (as the client).
- **Scope:** The four backend processing-pipeline analytics events (emission, payloads, idempotent, fire-and-forget, PII). NOT the pipeline logic (observed), NOT the client analytics (prior), NOT the sink/warehouse infra (assumed).
- **Stack:** Node + TypeScript; observes the pipeline; backend telemetry sink; fire-and-forget. Adjust to the repo's structure.

===========================================================================================================>>>>>>>>



# ANALYTICS: notification_opted_in, notification_received
# Product: maya-viewer (Backend Processing Pipeline — client)
# Scope: Analytics — notification opt-in + receipt events
# Priority: Medium

---

## Task Description

Instrument two analytics events around job/processing push notifications: `notification_opted_in` when the
user grants/enables notifications, and `notification_received` when a notification is delivered to the device.
Together these measure the notification funnel — how many users opt in, and whether notifications actually
arrive — for the "your model is ready / failed" processing notifications.

- [ ] Emit `notification_opted_in` when the user opts in / grants notification permission
- [ ] Emit `notification_received` when a notification is received on the device
- [ ] Fire each on the actual event edge (permission granted; notification delivered), not on screen render
- [ ] Carry consistent properties so the events join the job/notification funnel

## Files to Inspect First

1. The notification/permission handling in the app — find where notification permission is requested/granted
   (e.g., a permission prompt flow) and where incoming push notifications are handled. If NO notification
   capability exists yet, this is a blocking dependency (see Assumptions) — these events instrument a feature
   that must exist to fire.
2. The push-notification integration (FCM / APNs / the project's push SDK) — find the callback/handler for a
   received notification (foreground and background/tapped paths) so `notification_received` fires at delivery.
3. The permission-request code path — find the point where the OS returns a granted result so
   `notification_opted_in` fires on the genuine grant, not merely on showing the prompt.
4. `lib/services/analytics_service.dart` (or equivalent) — match the existing event-logging signature and how
   ids like `capture_session_id` / `job_id` and `device_type` are sourced.
5. The job status / notification payload — confirm whether a received notification carries a `job_id` so
   `notification_received` can attribute delivery to a job.

### Tech note (task-specific)
`notification_opted_in` fires on the OS permission GRANT result (authorized), not on prompt display and not on
denial. `notification_received` fires from the push handler when a notification is actually delivered — cover the
handler paths the SDK exposes (foreground receipt, background, and open/tap) per the confirmed intent (see Assumptions).

## Analytics Events

Event: `notification_opted_in`
Trigger: when the user grants notification permission / enables notifications (the granted edge).
Properties:
  - device_type: "mobile"
  - platform: "ios" | "android"
  - source?: string    // where opt-in happened (e.g., "processing_wait", "settings") if determinable
Notes: fire once on transition to granted. Do NOT fire on prompt shown or on denial. If the user re-grants after
revoking (via OS settings), firing again on the new grant is acceptable (confirm).

Event: `notification_received`
Trigger: when a notification is delivered to the device.
Properties:
  - device_type: "mobile"
  - platform: "ios" | "android"
  - job_id?: string          // if the notification payload carries it
  - notification_type?: string  // e.g., "processing_complete" | "processing_failed" if distinguishable
  - app_state?: "foreground" | "background"  // receipt context, if determinable
Notes: fire on genuine delivery/receipt. Decide and document whether "received" means delivered-while-foregrounded,
delivered-in-background, and/or opened/tapped — define it consistently (see Assumptions).

Match the existing event-logging signature and id sourcing so these join the notification/job funnel.

## Implementation Instructions

### Step 1: Confirm the notification capability exists (or flag the dependency)
Verify the app actually has notification permission handling and a push receipt handler. If it does not, these
analytics cannot meaningfully fire — surface this as a blocked dependency rather than adding dead event code
(see Assumptions). The rest of the steps assume the capability exists.

### Step 2: Emit notification_opted_in on the grant edge
Hook the permission-request result. When the OS reports permission GRANTED (authorized), emit
`notification_opted_in` once with platform/source. Do not emit on prompt display or on a denied result. Guard so
a single grant does not emit multiple times (e.g., don't fire on every app launch that re-checks status).

### Step 3: Emit notification_received in the push handler
In the push notification handler(s), emit `notification_received` when a notification is delivered, attaching
`job_id`/`notification_type` from the payload and `app_state` where determinable. Cover the handler entry points
per the confirmed definition of "received" (foreground / background / tapped).

### Step 4: Populate properties consistently
Source `platform`/`device_type` consistently; pull `job_id`/`notification_type` from the payload; set `app_state`
from the receipt context. Reuse the funnel id convention used by the other job events.

## What NOT to Change

- Do NOT build the notification feature itself here (permission flow, push registration, delivery) — this task
  instruments existing behavior. If the feature is missing, flag it; do not stub fake events.
- Do NOT fire `notification_opted_in` on prompt display or on denial — only on the granted edge.
- Do NOT fire `notification_received` on screen render or per rebuild — only from the push handler on delivery.
- Do NOT change the analytics service signature or invent a new logging path.
- Do NOT emit duplicate opt-in events on every launch/status re-check.

## Edge Cases to Handle

- [ ] Permission denied → `notification_opted_in` does NOT fire.
- [ ] Permission prompt shown but dismissed without a decision → no opt-in event.
- [ ] Permission previously granted, app relaunched (status re-checked) → do NOT re-fire opt-in on every launch;
      fire only on a genuine transition to granted.
- [ ] User revokes in OS settings then re-grants → firing opt-in on the new grant is acceptable (confirm).
- [ ] Notification received in foreground vs background vs opened-from-tap → fire per the defined "received"
      semantics; set `app_state` accordingly; avoid double-counting one delivery across multiple handlers.
- [ ] Notification payload without a `job_id` → still fire `notification_received` with `job_id` omitted.
- [ ] Duplicate delivery of the same push (platform can redeliver) → avoid double `notification_received` for one
      logical delivery where detectable (e.g., dedupe on a message id if present) — confirm feasibility.
- [ ] Platform differences (iOS vs Android handler models) → both platforms emit correctly per their SDK paths.

## Constraints

- `notification_opted_in` fires only on the genuine permission-granted edge, once per grant transition.
- `notification_received` fires from the push handler on actual delivery, per a single documented definition of
  "received"; one logical delivery must not be counted multiple times across handler paths.
- Events are side effects of the real permission/delivery events, not tied to UI render.
- Properties (platform, device_type, job_id, type, app_state) sourced consistently; funnel id reused.
- No fabricated events for a non-existent feature — if notifications aren't implemented, the dependency is flagged.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] `notification_opted_in` fires once when notification permission is granted, with platform (+ source if known)
- [ ] `notification_opted_in` does NOT fire on prompt display, on denial, or on every relaunch status re-check
- [ ] `notification_received` fires on genuine notification delivery from the push handler, per the defined semantics
- [ ] `notification_received` carries `job_id`/`notification_type`/`app_state` when available
- [ ] One logical delivery is not double-counted across foreground/background/tap handlers
- [ ] Both iOS and Android emit correctly per their handler models
- [ ] No opt-in duplicates on relaunch; re-grant-after-revoke behavior is as confirmed
- [ ] Events use the existing analytics signature and join the job/notification funnel
- [ ] If the notification capability is absent, the dependency is flagged (no dead/stub event code shipped)
- [ ] No analyzer errors (`flutter analyze` passes clean)
- [ ] No runtime exceptions in permission and push-handler paths
- [ ] Code reviewed against "What NOT to Change" — no notification feature built here, no render-tied firing

## Testing Instructions

1. Trigger the permission prompt and GRANT; confirm one `notification_opted_in` with correct platform/source.
2. Trigger the prompt and DENY; confirm no opt-in event. Dismiss without deciding; confirm no event.
3. Relaunch the app with permission already granted; confirm opt-in does NOT re-fire.
4. (If supported) revoke in OS settings and re-grant; confirm the confirmed re-grant behavior.
5. Send a test push while the app is foregrounded; confirm one `notification_received` with `app_state:"foreground"`
   and `job_id`/`type` from the payload.
6. Send a test push while backgrounded, then tap it; confirm the delivery is counted once per the defined semantics
   (not double-counted across background + tap handlers).
7. Send a push without `job_id`; confirm `notification_received` fires with `job_id` omitted.
8. If message-id dedupe is feasible, send a duplicate delivery; confirm one event.
9. Verify both iOS and Android emit correctly.
10. Run `flutter analyze`; confirm zero issues.

## Assumptions

- KEY DEPENDENCY: Assumed a push-notification capability EXISTS in the app (permission flow + push registration +
  a receipt handler) for processing notifications ("model ready / failed"). Nothing earlier in this phase built it
  — the client tracks jobs by POLLING `GET /jobs/:jobId`. If notifications are NOT yet implemented, these two
  events instrument a non-existent feature; the notification feature (and likely a server-side push on job
  completion) must be built first. Confirm the feature exists or scope it before this task can meaningfully ship.
- Assumed `notification_opted_in` maps to the OS permission-granted edge. If "opted in" means a separate in-app
  toggle/preference distinct from OS permission, the trigger point changes — confirm which is authoritative.
- Assumed a single documented definition of `notification_received` is needed (delivered-foreground vs delivered-
  background vs opened/tapped). Different products count these differently; confirm the intended definition so the
  event isn't ambiguous or double-counted. Note iOS silent/background delivery may not invoke app code reliably,
  limiting what "received" can capture there.
- Assumed the notification payload can carry `job_id`/`notification_type`; if not, those properties are omitted and
  attribution to a specific job is limited — confirm the payload shape (a server-side concern).
- Assumed platform push redelivery may occur; dedupe on a message id only if the payload provides one — confirm feasibility.
- Assumed `capture_session_id`/`job_id` funnel conventions from the other job events apply here.




The dependency to surface loudly before anyone builds this — flagged as the key assumption — is that nothing in this phase has built push notifications. Every client task so far assumed polling (GET /jobs/:jobId with the adaptive poller) as the way users learn their model is ready. There's been no permission-opt-in flow, no push registration, and no server-side push-on-completion. So notification_opted_in and notification_received instrument a feature that may simply not exist yet. If it doesn't, the correct move is to flag the dependency and not ship dead event-emitting code wired to handlers that never fire — instrumenting a phantom feature produces a permanently-zero funnel that looks like a bug forever. The notification capability (and probably a backend push trigger on job completion) has to exist first.
The two definitional traps, both flagged:
notification_opted_in must fire on the granted edge, not the prompt. The common mistake is firing when the permission dialog is shown, which measures "how many users we asked," not "how many opted in" — and it double-counts on every relaunch that re-checks permission status. It fires once, on the genuine transition to granted; never on display, never on denial, never on a status re-check at launch.
notification_received needs one documented definition. "Received" is genuinely ambiguous across platforms and products: delivered while the app is foregrounded, delivered silently in the background, or opened-from-tap are three different things, often handled by three different SDK callbacks. Without a single agreed definition, the same delivery gets counted once, twice, or not at all depending on app state, and the metric becomes meaningless. There's also a hard platform limit worth knowing: iOS background/silent delivery may not reliably invoke app code, so on iOS "received" can't always be captured — which caps what this event can honestly measure there.
This is a rare task in the recent run that's mostly self-contained on the analytics side but exposes a genuine missing feature rather than just a missing decision. It doesn't add to the backend contract pile so much as reveal a gap in the phase's scope: there's a whole notification subsystem (client permission flow, push registration, server-side completion push, payload shape carrying job_id) implied by these two events and not yet accounted for anywhere.
Given that, the most useful next step probably isn't another analytics prompt — it's deciding whether notifications are actually in scope for this phase. If they are, there's a small cluster of real tasks to scope (server push-on-completion, client permission + registration, the payload contract) before these events have anything to fire on. If they're not, these two events should wait.
Want me to scope that notification subsystem — starting with the server-side push-on-job-completion and the client permission/registration flow — so these analytics events have a real feature underneath them? Or, since this is now the several-th task hinging on shared pipeline definitions (job states, job_id funnel, completion triggers), assemble the Backend Processing Pipeline contract that would also pin the notification payload and completion-trigger points?

# NEW: Test — Worker Processes an Uploaded Bundle End-to-End (Stub Photogrammetry Output)

- [ ] Hermetic (stub engine + fakes) + deterministic; sabotage-checked (broken pipeline → test fails)

## Files to Inspect First

> Adjust these paths to the real repo layout. They reflect the assumed conventions in the Assumptions section — confirm before the agent edits anything.

1. The processing-pipeline state-machine task — the stages/transitions + the reconstruction-engine adapter interface (to STUB)
2. The quality-report + project-completion-update tasks — the completion side effects to assert

## Test Design

### Setup (hermetic)
- Fakes: DB (job/project records), queue (deliver the job), artifact storage (capture uploaded artifacts/report), analytics sink (capture events). STUB the reconstruction engine adapter to return DETERMINISTIC canned outputs per stage (a fake mesh/texture/optimized model + quality metrics).
- A fixture BUNDLE: an ingested job in QUEUED with a fixture manifest + a few photos + sidecars (minimal but representative).

### Happy path
1. Enqueue the QUEUED job → run the worker.
2. Assert the stage sequence QUEUED→PROCESSING→TEXTURING→OPTIMIZING→COMPLETED, each PERSISTED (observe the status at each transition).
3. Assert completion side effects:
   - `quality_report.json` generated (from the stub metrics) + uploaded to the fake artifact store.
   - Project updated: `latestCompletedJobId = job.id`, `status = COMPLETED` (after outputs ready).
   - Analytics: processing_started → stage_changed (per transition) → processing_completed, with correct payloads/attempt.
   - Artifacts (stub mesh/textures/LODs) present in the fake store; the report references them.

### Failure path
1. Configure the stub engine to FAIL at a stage (e.g. TEXTURING).
2. Assert: job → FAILED with {stage, error}; processing_failed emitted; NO COMPLETED; NO project `status=COMPLETED`; report/artifacts absent or partial per policy.

### Idempotency (light)
1. Re-run the same job (duplicate delivery / retry-completed) → assert the same final state, NO duplicate artifacts (same keyed paths overwritten) or duplicate terminal events.

### Determinism
- The stub returns fixed outputs; fakes are deterministic; no real reconstruction/timing. The test is repeatable.

## What NOT to Do (test)

- Do NOT use real photogrammetry/reconstruction — stub the engine adapter (deterministic canned outputs).
- Do NOT use real infra — hermetic fakes (DB, queue, artifact storage, analytics sink).
- Do NOT assert only the final status — assert the full transition sequence AND all completion side effects.
- Do NOT skip the failure path — cover stub-fails → FAILED + processing_failed + no completion.
- Do NOT make it non-deterministic — fixed stub outputs; hermetic; repeatable.
- Do NOT let a broken pipeline pass — include sabotage checks (skipped stage / missing side effect → fail).
- Do NOT couple to real timing — the stub returns promptly.

## Edge Cases to Cover

- [ ] Happy path → all stages + all side effects (report, project update, analytics, artifacts).
- [ ] Failure at a stage → FAILED + processing_failed + no COMPLETED/report/project-COMPLETED.
- [ ] Re-run (duplicate delivery / retry) → same result, no duplicate artifacts/events (idempotent).
- [ ] Report references the stub artifacts (consistency).
- [ ] Project update happens AFTER outputs are ready (a COMPLETED project has fetchable outputs).
- [ ] Analytics fired per transition with correct attempt/stage/durations.
- [ ] Empty/invalid bundle → early FAILED with a clear error (not a crash).
- [ ] Concurrent jobs (optional) → independent processing, correct per-job side effects.

## Constraints

- The test MUST run the FULL worker pipeline end-to-end with a STUBBED engine (deterministic outputs) and hermetic fakes.
- It MUST assert the persisted stage sequence AND every completion side effect (report+upload, project update, analytics, artifacts).
- It MUST cover the failure path (stub fails → FAILED + processing_failed + no completion side effects).
- It MUST include a light idempotency check (re-run → same result, no duplicates).
- It MUST be deterministic and repeatable; it MUST include sabotage checks proving it fails on a broken pipeline.

## Acceptance Criteria

- [ ] The test runs a fixture bundle through QUEUED→…→COMPLETED (each stage persisted) with the stubbed engine.
- [ ] It asserts all completion side effects: report generated+uploaded, project updated, analytics fired, artifacts produced (referenced by the report).
- [ ] The failure path asserts FAILED + processing_failed + no completion side effects.
- [ ] The idempotency check asserts a re-run yields the same result with no duplicate artifacts/events.
- [ ] The test is hermetic, deterministic, and sabotage-checked (fails on skipped stage / missing side effect).
- [ ] Typecheck + tests pass (and the test fails on the seeded regressions).

## Testing Instructions (meta — validate the test itself)

1. Run against the correct pipeline → happy path + failure path + idempotency all pass.
2. Sabotage: make the pipeline skip a stage (e.g. jump PROCESSING→OPTIMIZING) → assert the sequence assertion FAILS.
3. Sabotage: skip the quality-report upload → assert the side-effect assertion FAILS.
4. Sabotage: skip the project update → assert that assertion FAILS.
5. Sabotage: suppress processing_completed → assert the analytics assertion FAILS.
6. Sabotage: make artifacts write to non-deterministic paths → assert the idempotency (no-duplicate) check FAILS.
7. Confirm determinism (repeat runs identical) and hermeticity (no real infra/engine).
8. Typecheck + suite → clean/pass.

## Assumptions

- **Stubbed engine:** Assumes the reconstruction is behind an adapter that can be stubbed with deterministic outputs (per the state-machine task's delegation). Blocked if not adapterized.
- **Starts from an ingested QUEUED job:** Assumes a fixture bundle can be set up as a QUEUED job (ingestion is separate/unbuilt). If ingestion exists, the test may start from the upload; else from a QUEUED job + fixture. Confirm.
- **Side effects exist:** Assumes the quality-report, project-update, and analytics tasks are built; this test asserts them together. 
- **Hermetic fakes:** Assumes fakes for DB/queue/artifact-storage/analytics (or a test harness). Confirm the harness.
- **Idempotency:** Assumes the idempotent-processing guarantees; the light check verifies no duplicates on re-run.
- **Scope:** The E2E worker test (stub engine, full pipeline, side effects, failure, idempotency, sabotage-checked). NOT the reconstruction engine (stubbed), NOT ingestion (separate; test starts from a QUEUED job), NOT the client.
- **Stack:** Node + TypeScript integration test; stub engine + hermetic fakes; deterministic. Adjust to the repo's structure.
